PREFACE


In the present work, rhyme effects are defined as conventional elements
of classical Latin poetry. This definition admittedly is an unconventional
one and collides with the axiom that quantitative poetry is unrhymed.
It is well known that classical Latin poetry contains repetitions of sound
in parallel metrical positions — e.g., similar syllables or words at consecutive verse endings — which have an effect comparable to medieval
or modern rhyme. Such repetitions would be considered as rhymes in a
non-quantitative poem. In classical Latin poetry, however, rhyme effects
are disregarded, explained away as unintentional side effects of inflection,
or criticized as prosaisms due to the corrupting influence of rhetoric.
While rhyme as a general phenomenon is not recognized, occasional
comments on special cases of rhyme do appear in the literature. These
publications usually concentrate on a single verse form, e.g., the
elegiac couplet, or on one particular poet, e.g., Lucretius or Vergil.
Even convincing examples of rhyme could therefore be dismissed as
curiosities without general significance.
The present investigation does not concentrate upon any metre or poet
in particular but is directed towards general phenomena. Examples of
rhyme represent a broad selection of authors, metres, and literary _genres,_
including passages from classical Greek poetry. In order to avoid faulty
or subjective judgments the critical approach to rhyme moreover is based
upon a systematic evaluation of ancient literary theory.
The data obtained from this combined analysis indicate that regular
repetitions of sound in quantitative poetry have the function of rhyme,
i.e., they must be considered conventional elements of poetic composition.
Some important points are:


_1. Evaluation of ancient literary theory_
a. Ancient rhetoricians, grammarians, and literary critics include rhyme
and rhyme-like effects among the figures of speech, which are accomplish

**6** PREFACE


ments of style. Rhyme effects therefore need not be explained away as
accidents of grammar and syntax.
b. According to ancient literary theory rhyming figures were copied
from poetry by the inventors of artistic prose, not _vice versa._ Rhyme
therefore need not be defined as 'rhetorical' but may be considered as
originally and essentially poetic.
c. Rhyming figures in ancient catalogues frequently are exemplified
from classical Greek and Latin poetry (including Homer and Sappho,
1.e., poetry older than artistic prose). Rhyme in poetry thus must have
been considered deliberate.


**2.** _Evaluation of poetic practice_
a. Examples of rhyming figures may readily be identified in random
selections from any classical Latin poet. It may be concluded that rhyme
was not avoided in poetry.
b. While rhyme effects in rhetorical theory are exemplified in isolation,
poems contain various rhyme effects in combination. The same rhymes
moreover may recur throughout long passages and entire poems.
Frequent rhyme patterns are: periodical repetition of identical or similar
sounds and words; arrangements in stanza-like groups; symmetrical
patterns.
c. End rhyme patterns moreover depend on a limited, conventional
repertory of line endings, which recur in almost any poem regardless of
author, metre (except for iambic and Ionic metres), subject, and period.
d. Comparisons of Latin epics with Homeric poems show similar
arrangements, comparable sequences of rhyme, and even similar rhyme
effects.


**3.** _Comparative analyses of end rhyme patterns_
a. In epic poetry acoustic replication (identity or similarity of verse
endings in parallel positions relative to entire books or poems) was
identified through manual analysis.
b. Regular runs of specific verse endings in _Aeneid I_ were identified
through analysis by computer.
c. Acoustic parallelism of end rhyme patterns in symmetrical poems
by Catullus was found through manual analysis.
None of these phenomena to my knowledge have been identified or
described to date. Although the parallels and regularities so far have not
converged into overall numerical arrangements, they do suggest that
rhyme effects, especially end rhymes, follow conventional patterns of
poetic composition.


PREFACE 7


It is hoped that the present study will be useful not only as a new approach
to the enjoyment of quantitative poetry, but also as a fresh tool for
literary and textual criticism. The evaluation of ancient comments on
sound repetition and the fully documented catalogue of rhyming figures
may also be helpful to students of speech and rhetoric, while comparisons
of classical rhyme effects with later systems of rhyme (for example, the
conceits of the Italian Renaissance) may be of interest to students of
comparative literature.


The present book has developed from my doctoral dissertation, _Patterns_
_of Sound Repetition in Classical Latin Poetry,_ and also contains material
from my master's thesis, _Rhyme in Classical Latin Poetry._ I wish to
thank Professor Robert P. Sonkowsky and Professor Roy A. Swanson for
their advice and their interest in my work.
I also wish to acknowledge valuable criticism and comments from
Professor Donald C. Swanson and Professor Viktor Poschl, as well as
from the editor of the series, _De Proprietatibus Litterarum,_ Professor
Cornelius H. van Schooneveld.
The computer analysis of _Aeneid I_ was done by my husband, Professor
Henry W. Guggenheimer, on the computer of the Polytechnic Institute of
Brooklyn. The analysis was based on a modified set of Professor Nathan
A. Greenberg's punched cards of _Aeneid I_ adapted by Mr. Stanley

INTRODUCTION


**La littérature antique était une**
**littérature à haute voix.**
**(Herescu)**


Classical Latin poetry, if read aloud, often seems to rhyme: Was such
an effect intended?
Generally this question is answered in the negative. A student collecting
information on classical Latin poetry will find that it is considered
unrhymed. Apparent rhymes usually are explained as unintentional side
effects of inflection, or as devices copied from artistic prose under the
influence of rhetoric. According to L. P. Wilkinson,


a tendency to actual rhyme could hardly fail to occur in an inflected
language, and it was promoted by the taste for parallelism.
TrapicjfflGvç and OHOIOTSXSUXOV were both Gorgianic figures, and
such rhyme as occurs in classical times is a chance by-product of
rhetoric. [1 ]


If rhetorical theory recognized and recommended rhyming devices,
the student may ask, where did these devices originate? The answer of
classical scholarship is a classical example of circular argument. It is
taken for granted that Gorgias, the inventor of artistic prose, copied his
figures from poetry, whereas poets avoided Gorgianic figures because
they were prosaic.
As K. Polheim remarks, Gorgias was criticized for introducing poetic
devices into prose :


Neben der Masslosigkeit seines Figurenstils, dem frostigen und
puerilen Wesen seiner Rede tadelte man an Gorgias, dass er die
Grenzen der poetischen und prosaischen Sprache verwische. Sein

On the other hand, the Gorgianic figures, if they occur in poetry, are

Aside from these considerations, rhyme and other repetitions of sound
in Latin often are associated with the ancient _carmina_ and therefore
considered as essentially preliterate, popular devices. "The jingle of
rustic incantations", as Wilkinson calls it, according to most authorities
was avoided in classical Latin poetry.
Thus instructed, the student might in the future, while noticing and

Nevertheless, such effects are by no means rare: Would accomplished
poets like Catullus and Vergil have been unable to avoid rustic jingles
and prosaisms in their works, if they had wished to do so? Also, why are
these 'rustic jingles' and 'prosaisms', even if they consist of metrically
unaccented syllables, often arranged in regular patterns?
As Maurice P. Cunningham remarks, "grammatical relation is expressed
in Latin primarily by morphological means", [13] while, from the grammatical standpoint, the word order is not rigidly fixed. In classical Latin
poetry slight metathesis of words, which would have destroyed the rhyme,
in many cases was technically possible. Otherwise non-rhyming synonyms might have replaced rhyming words. Given the flexibility of diction
and word order in Latin, is it not reasonable to suppose that the rhyme
patterns of classical Latin poetry might easily have been broken by
substitutions of words and the rearrangement of sentences?
It is instructive to compare the supposedly unintentional rhyme
patterns of classical Latin poetry with post-classical, rhymed poems. A
comparison with regularly rhymed, non-quantitative poetry shows that
the difference of the rhyme effects is sometimes not very great. Catullus's
couplets of metrically unstressed suffixes are paralleled in medieval
poetry, e.g.:


inde cum multas
referret causas
subiunxit totwm
esse infernwm
accinctum densw

These examples show that, except for the quantitative metre, ancient
and modern patterns of rhyme in some cases are comparable. But
although rhyme in simple and extended form is recognized as an important element of acoustic structure in post-classical poetry, similar
acoustic patterns are disregarded if they occur in a classical Latin poem.
On what grounds, the student may ask himself, must it be assumed that
rhymes are inaudible in classical, quantitative poetry?
Standard authorities associate deliberately rhymed Latin poetry with
the Middle Ages, i.e., with non-quantitative poetry. The terminology of
poetry and rhyme, however, is far from clear and instead of enlightening
the student may even lead him into greater confusion.
As a case in point, Norden's contradictory statements on medieval
church hymns may be noted. In Norden's Appendix on the history of
rhyme, the hymns are first described as rhyming prose and later as
rhymed poetry:


Bezeichnend scheint mir auch der Name, den die mittelalterliche
Kirche für die dem Halleluja untergelegten Gesangstexte wählte: sie
hiessen _**prosae.**_
Da nun also eine erhebliche Wesensverschiedenheit der hochrhetorischen Predigt und des feierlichen Kirchengesangs nicht
existiert hat, so sind wir berechtigt oder vielmehr genötigt, beide in
betreff ihrer am meisten charakteristischen Erscheinungsform,
nämlich des Reims, in engste Beziehung zueinander zu setzen, oder

   - mit anderen Worten — den Reim der hohen Prosa mit dem der
getragenen Poesie für identisch zu erklären Ganz wie in der
Kunstprosa seit Gorgias findet sich der Reim in den Hymnen nur
an besonders pathetischen Stellen: die Verfasser dieser Hymnen
sind sich also bewusst gewesen, im Reim eine fakultative, nicht eine
obligatorische Zier _**(a%f\\ia, figurä)**_ der Verse zu besitzen. [23 ]

**20** SOUND REPETITIONS IN ANCIENT LITERARY THEORY


Despite the optional character of their rhymes, Norden considers the
medieval church hymns as the first models of modern rhymed poetry.
In Norden's opinion "the fact must be considered as certain that rhyme
in the poetry of modern peoples made its actual appearance by transmission from Latin hymn song " [M] Norden does not state clearly,
however, at which point rhyme from a rhetorical figure turned into a
device of poetry.
Norden's definition of rhyme as a rhetorical device is correct insofar
as sound repetitions are not elements of the quantitative metre. The same
definition of rhyme, however, strictly speaking also applies to rhymes in
post-classical poetry, which has no quantitative metre at all. Therefore
from Norden's viewpoint a modern rhymed poem might be defined, not
as poetry, but rather as a piece of elevated prose decorated in regular
intervals with certain embellishments of rhetoric.
In order to be entirely consistent, one would thus either have to define
all rhymes as rhetorical and all non-quantitative poetry as prose: With
the same degree of logic one might define classical quantitative poetry as
prose because it is not regularly rhymed in stanzas.
It seems therefore that the modern division of rhymes into 'rhetorical'
and 'poetic' devices, in quantitative and nonquantitative poetry respectively, needlessly complicates the issue without clarifying the actual
aesthetic connotations of rhyme. To avoid the embarassment resulting
from modern terminology, the student may decide to concentrate on
ancient authors, who need not be suspected of carrying modern notions
back into antiquity.
No ancient source claims that rhyme was originally a device of prose.
On the contrary ancient literary theorists mention figures of sound
repetition among the decorations of diction copied from poetry by the
inventors of artistic prose. Because of their rhyme effect these devices
are recommended for special cases, either as ornaments or to add emphasis to the speech. Moreover repetitions of sound serve in the construction of acoustic units, e.g., in marking beginnings and endings of
coordinated clauses in prose or of metrical units in poetry, and thus
result in harmony and acoustic structure. Rhyming figures accordingly
are counted among the highlights ( _lumina_ ) and elegancies ( _exornationes)_
of style.


in especially moving passages: the creators of these hymns accordingly were aware
that they possessed in rhyme an optional rather than an obligatory ornament ('figure')
of verse." (Norden, p. 861).
u Norden, p. 813.


INTRODUCTION 21


In ancient treatises on style rhyming figures often are exemplified by
quotations from classical literature. Although these examples may be
taken from poetry or artistic prose, rhymes are considered suitable for
poets and epideictic speakers rather than for politicians and lawyers in
court. If at all used in an actual case, the ancient principle is that rhyme
effects must be appropriate, not monotonous, not forced upon the sense,
and rare.
Most extant comments on rhyming figures are contained in treatises
on rhetoric. These handbooks aim to prepare the student for effective
speaking in court, where sober and serious language is called for. Although
a public speaker might use occasional rhyme effects to advantage, he
might also ruin his speech by too many or too obvious rhymes.
Authors therefore recommend caution:


haec tria genera exornationum, quorum unum in similiter cadentibus, alterum in similiter desinentibus verbis, tertium in adnominatione positum est, perraro sumenda sunt cum in veritate dicimus 25


(actori verae causae) elocutionis figuris modus adhibendus, et iis
maxime... in quibus paribus paria redduntur aut simili casu aut sono
determinantur; in his etiam, quae iteratione verbi aut repetitione
aut aliocumque huius modi motu accidunt. [26 ]


In serious cases rhyming figures are felt to be entirely out of place:


... ubi vero atrocitate, invidia, miseratione pugnandum est, quis
ferat contrapositis et pariter cadentibus et consimilibus irascentem,
flentem, rogantem? cum nimia in his rebus cura verborum deroget
adfectibus fidem, et ubicumque ars ostentatur, Veritas abesse
videatur. [27 ]


It would be a double fallacy to conclude from these precepts to orators
that rhyme in classical antiquity (1) was essentially a device of prose


**25** **"These last three types of figures, the first of which consists in similar cases of**
**words, the second in similar endings, the third in plays upon words, should be used**
**very sparingly if we speak in an actual cause** **" (** _**Auctor ad Herennium**_ **, IV, 22, 32).**
**M** **"[A speaker in an actual cause] must use figures of speech in moderation, especially**
**those... in which similar [parts of speech] are balanced against each other or end in**
**similar cases or sounds; in those likewise which result from reduplication or repetition**
**of a word or any other trope of this kind." (Aquila Romanus, § 42).**
**a'** **"But where one must fight by horror, hate, or pity, who would represent, by**
**balanced clauses and similar cases and exact correspondences, a furious, or a weeping,**
**or a begging person? because in these matters too great concern with words lessens**
**the credibility of the emotions, and wherever art is displayed sincerity seems to be**
**absent." (Quintilian,** _**Institutio Oratorio,**_ **IX, 3, 102).**


**22** **SOUND REPETITIONS IN ANCIENT LITERARY THEORY**


which (2) had to be avoided by poets. One may rather come to the
opposite conclusion. Rhyme apparently was felt to be far from 'prosaic'
since it had to be avoided by serious speakers of prose.
It may even be argued that in classical literary theory the concept of
'rhetorical rhyme' does not exist. All sources agree that the origin of
rhyme is poetry, that its nature is poetic, that its uses are mainly for poets
and epideictic speakers, and that _vitium_ from rhyming figures occurs
mainly in prose. Thus the ancient authorities encourage a modern
student to recognize and appreciate rhyme effects in quantitative poetry
as a legitimate element of poetic composition.


II


THE ORIGIN OF RHYMING FIGURES


legendi etiampoetae... (Crassus).... poetas
omnino quasi alia quadam lingua locutos
non conor attingere. (Antonius).
(Cicero, _De Oratore)_


According to ancient authorities the origin of rhyming figures is not prose
but poetry, a theory which tallies with the historical priority of poetry
over artistic prose. Gorgias, the founder of professional oratory, is said
to have copied rhyme effects from poetry in order to achieve "numerus"
or rhythmical structure.

Ancient literary criticism shows that the Gorgianic figures, despite
their use in prose, were felt to retain part of their original, poetic quality.
This ambivalent character of prose rhyme is reflected in ambivalent critical
evaluations. On the one hand rhyme effects are carefully catalogued,
classified, and recommended to students of rhetoric; on the other hand,
the poetic effect of rhyming figures in prose is often considered out of
place. One may add that the doubtful reputation of Gorgias himself
sometimes is more aggravating to ancient critics than the poetic origin of
his figures.

It is generally agreed that the first poets were the creators not only of
poetic forms but of artistic literary expression in general. Ancient poetry
exemplifies rhetorical perfection as well as poetic genius. It is therefore
not surprising to find Homer described as the father of eloquence:


...nos rite coepturi ab Homero videmur. hie enim...omnibus
eloquentiae partibus exemplum et ortum dedit. hunc nemo in
magnis rebus sublimitate, in parvis proprietate superavit. idem
laetus [latus?] ac pressus, iucundus et gravis, turn copia turn brevitate mirabilis, nec poetica modo, sed oratoria virtute eminentissimus. [1 ]


1 "... it seems that we will do well to start with Homer, for he... has given the model
and origin to all parts of eloquence. No one has excelled his sublimity in great, or his


**24** SOUND REPETITIONS IN ANCIENT LITERARY THEORY


In Cicero's opinion Homer would not have praised Odysseus and Nestor
for their eloquence if he had not been an orator himself as well as a
poet. [2 ]

According to Quintilian, oratory in all its aspects was perfected by
Homer who, besides paying attention to word choice, ideas, and overall
organization, also used figures of speech:


quid? in verbis, sententiis, figuris, dispositione totius operis nonne
humani ingenii modum excedit? [3 ]


For Demetrius even the periodic prose structure, as introduced by
Gorgias and Isocrates, might be compared to the compactness of the
Homeric hexameter. [4 ]

Homer is by no means the only poet recommended to students of
oratory. In Latin literature Homer's excellence is paralleled by Vergil's. [5 ]

Future orators are advised to study the poets, [6] particularly those who
excel in qualities of style such as Euripides [7] and Lucan. [8 ]

Horace, praised by Ovid as "numerosus", [9] is the only lyric poet whom
Quintilian recommends to future orators, mainly for the qualities of his
figures and his diction:


nam et insurgit aliquando et plenus est iucunditatis et gratiae et
varius figuris et verbis felicissime audax. [10 ]


Occasionally an orator may even insert verses from poetry into a
speech, not only to show his erudition but also to make his style more
attractive. [11 ]

Although oratory for its artistic devices thus depends on poetry,
artistic prose must not be allowed to develop into a poem. The future

**in poetic but also in oratorical excellence."** _**{ibid.,**_ **X, 1, 46).**

THE ORIGIN OF RHYMING FIGURES **25**


orator should be thoroughly acquainted with poetry, but he may not
imitate the poets' wide range of diction and their indulgence in figures. [12 ]

Especial caution is necessary in the matter of _numerus,_ or prose rhythm,
which to some extent is an imitation of _numerus_ in poetry, i.e., metre:


nec in numeris magis quam in reliquis ornamentis orationis, eadem
cum faciamus quae poetae, effugimus tamen in oratione poematis
similitudinem. [13 ]


_Numerus,_ which may refer to metre in poetry, is a technical term of
complex meaning. Like its English derivative, 'numbers', _numerus_ may
denote rhythmical or metrical regularity and harmony in general. But
_numerus_ is also mentioned more specifically as a concomitant of the
Gorgianic figures, i.e., artistic speech patterns involving parallel constructions and rhyming figures. [14 ]

As in other respects, poetry is the example of artistic prose in the matter
of _numerus._ Despite the fundamental difference of metre, poetry and
prose have comparable acoustic structure based on similar, coordinated
units of composition. The perfectly regular repetition of metrical or
rhythmical sequences in poetry is imitated in oratory by the less regular
balanced clauses of the periodic style.
The principle of composition in oratory thus is comparable to that of
verse. Both periodic prose and poetry are built from more or less regular
acoustic units which must be marked off by audible divisions. In classical
times these important breaks at endings of acoustic units are a feature
shared by poetry and artistic prose. [15] Originally, however, acoustic
breaks were elements of poetical, or musical, composition:


versus enim veteres illi in hac soluta oratione propemodum, hoc
est, numeros quosdam nobis esse adhibendos putaverunt: interspirationis enim, non defetigationis nostrae neque librariorum
notis, sed verborum et sententiarum modo interpunctas clausulas in
orationibus esse voluerunt; idque princeps Isocrates instituisse
fertur, ut inconditam antiquorum dicendi consuetudinem delectationis atque aurium causa, quem ad modum scribit discipulus eius
Naucrates, numeris astringeret. namque haec duo musici, qui erant


_**12**_ _**ibid.,**_ **X, 1, 27-8.**

**201).**
_**14**_ _**ibid., 52,**_ **175-6.**

**26** **SOUND REPETITIONS IN ANCIENT LITERARY THEORY**


quondam idem poetae, machinati ad voluptatem sunt, versum
atque cantum, ut et verborum numero et vocum modo delectatione
vincerent aurium satietatem. haec igitur duo, vocis dico moderationem et verborum conclusionem, quoad orationis severitas pati
posset, a poetica ad eloquentiam traducenda duxerunt. [16 ]


While _numerus,_ in the sense of rhythmical regularity, in oratory was a
late addition, [17] poetry had always had a natural affinity to numbers, not
only in the acoustic but also in the mathematical sense. According to
Quintilian the ancient poets, who were at the same time musicians, also
understood astronomy and things supernatural. [18] Similar references to
the mathematical and magical aspects of poetry and music may be found
in Martianus Capella. [19] This connection of poetry and music with
mathematics and religion suggests the Pythagorean concept of numerical
regularity and harmony as a structural principle of the universe.
It may therefore be unjust to classify the formulaic, heavily rhymed
Latin _carmina_ with their extreme acoustic parallelism as 'rustic jingles',
as Wilkinson does (cf. p. 15). These rhymed incantations perhaps may
be considered as musical sequences arranged to a magic numerical
system. If comparable, regular sequences of rhyme occur in quantitative
Greek and Latin poetry of secular content, these effects might correspond
to numerical patterns still known to poets but used without their former
magical implications.
According to ancient sources the invention of artistic prose is associated
with Sicily. The beginnings of rhetorical theory go back to Empedocles
of Agrigentum and the Sicilian orators Corax and Tisias, [20] while the


**"** **"Indeed these ancient authorities felt that in such prose we should employ verse**
**almost, that is to say, a certain rhythmical regularity: they wanted that in oratory there**
**should be pauses, of breathing to be sure rather than of our exhaustion, and marked,**
**not by the scribes' punctuation, but through the pattern of words and sentences;**
**Isocrates reportedly was the first to teach this method, in order to tie together the**
**ancients' random manner of speech by regular rhythm and also to please the audience,**
**just as his student Naucrates writes. In fact the musicians, who formerly were poets**
**as well, devised these two things, verse and song, for pleasure, so that they might**
**convince a weary audience by delighting it with the regular rhythm of words and the**
**pattern of sounds. It was believed that these two things, then, I mean the regulation**
**of sound and the conclusion of phrases, as far as was compatible with the seriousness**
**of oratory, should be transferred from poesy to rhetoric." (Cicero,** _**De Oratore,**_ **III,**
**44, 173-4).**

THE ORIGIN OF RHYMING FIGURES **27**


professional introduction of figures into oratory is generally attributed
to Gorgias of Leontini.
The Gorgianic figures were intended as imitations of poetry. Syntactic
parallelism and balanced _cola,_ closing with rhymed cadences and resulting
in rhythmical regularity, were among the most striking characteristics of
the Gorgianic style, which was very similar to verse. [21] The introduction
of figures sometimes was attributed (in Cicero's opinion, erroneously) to
Isocrates, but he, likewise, was said to have copied them from poetry:


nam qui Isocratem maxime mirantur, hoc in eius summis laudibus
ferunt, quod verbis solutis numeros primum adiunxerit. cum enim
videret oratores cum severitate audiri, poetas autem cum voluptate,
turn dicitur numeros secutus, quibus etiam in oratione uteretur,
cum iucunditatis causa turn ut varietas occurreret satietati. [22 ]


In Latin literature the chronological priority of poetry over prose is less
obvious because Greek oratory and poetry were introduced together and
taught within the general framework of literary studies. [23] Native Latin
verse, on the other hand, apparently had antiquarian rather than literary
interest.
Poetry always was studied and often practised by the rhetoricians.
According to Suetonius the same teachers gave courses in both literature
and oratory. [24] Victorinus defines the rhetorician as a man who "teaches
literature and passes on the theory of eloquence". [26] Suetonius notes that
Aurelius Opillus taught philosophy, rhetoric, and literature, [26] while
P. Valerius Cato [27] and Q. Remmius Palaemon [28] besides teaching literature

Such testimony makes the audience of Gorgias appear in a doubtful
light, but it helps one to understand the bad reputation of the Gorgianic
figures, which seems hardly warranted by considerations of literary taste.
Obviously the main argument against artistic prose was ethical rather
than aesthetic.
Roman officials feared the demagogical power of rhetoric; the teaching
of eloquence in Rome was viewed with distrust and periodically prohibited. [48 ]

Even in Cicero's time some orators deliberately avoided _**numerus**_ and
reproached those who were using it for legitimate purposes:


hoc _**[scil.,**_ the fact that _**numeri**_ are suspect] freti isti et ipsa infracta
et amputata loquuntur et eos vituperant qui apta et finita pronuntiant; si inanibus verbis levibusque sententiis, iure; sin probae res,
lecta verba, quid est cur claudere aut insistere orationem malint
quam cum sententia pariter excurrere? hie enim invidiosus numerus
nihil adfert aliud nisi ut sit apte verbis comprehensa sententia 49

THE NATURE OF RHYMING FIGURES


**arnica est enim similitudo, dissimilitudo**
**odiosa atque contraria.**
**(Boethius,** _**De Inst. Musica)**_


Rhyming figures are complementary to metre or prose rhythm insofar as
they help define 'cola', i.e., regular elements of acoustic structure, such
as verse lines in poetry or balanced clauses in periodic prose. While
quantitative metre and rhythmical prose patterns determine the shape and
extent of the entire _colon,_ rhyming figures usually emphasize important
parts of _cola,_ especially endings and beginnings. Some figures, however,
may include a more considerable part of the _colon_ or even extend over
entire _cola._
The majority of rhyming figures are sound repetitions based upon
formal parallelism or antithesis (both of which also constitute figures of
speech in rhetorical analysis). Although parallelism and antithesis have
logical implications, they also have formal aspects which may be
deliberately utilized to achieve rhythmical structure.
It is a characteristic of statements in parallel or antithetical form that
they require subdivisions _{cola, caesa)_ of equal or similar length [1] and
similar grammatical form. Since grammatical and acoustic similarities in
Greek and Latin inflection often coincide, regular sound repetition may
be an essential or incidental element of these structures.
Because of the dual function of parallelism and antithesis as both
logical and formal principles, it is not always possible to separate figures
of sound from figures of sense, and in some cases a figure is variously
classified by different authors. In this study the emphasis is on the
acoustic aspects of parallel and antithetical arrangements.
Some rhyming figures by definition are independent of parallel or
antithetical structures. Reduplication of words, several cases or inflectional
forms of the same word, derivatives from the same root, and puns belong

**1** **Quintilian, IX, 4, 22; Aquila, § 18; Martianus Capella, V, 528, 173G.**


THE NATURE OF RHYMING FIGURES **33**


in this category. Sound repetition in these figures does not occur in any
specified order or position relative to the _colon._ All the same even this
type of rhyme often is used in patterns which emphasize the acoustic
structure of _cola,_ e.g., at endings or beginnings.
The following ancient comments on the nature of rhyming figures are
quoted according to subject matter rather than according to chronology.
This method partly is suggested by the lack of historical information even
on well-known authors (e.g., _Auctor ad Herennium,_ "Longinus", and
Martianus Capella). It is true that a roughly chronological order of
sources all the same might be established, but it would not reflect a
development of critical thought.
In a period of literary activity which extends from Aristotle to Boethius
one might expect major developments and differences of critical opinion.
As far as figures of sound repetition are concerned this is not the case.
On the contrary discussions of rhyming figures are remarkably conventional throughout the entire period. Except for the increasing number
and variety of technical terms in later works, there seems to be little
development in the critical approach to rhyming figures.
The various kinds and degrees of rhyme usually are listed under
decorations of style. In Cicero's _De Oratore,_ Crassus offers a general
survey of conventional rhyming figures:


... ad venustatem ipsam tractatio. nam et geminatio verborum
habet interdum vim, leporem alias, et paulum immutatum verbum
atque deflexum et eiusdem verbi crebra turn a primo repetitio, turn
in extremum conversio et in eadem verba impetus et concursio et
adiunctio et progressio et eiusdem verbi crebrius positi quaedam
distinctio et revocatio verbi et ilia, quae similiter desinunt aut quae
cadunt similiter aut quae paribus paria referuntur aut quae sunt
inter se similia. est etiam gradatio quaedam et conversio... et
contrarium... et quod in multis casibus ponitur... [2 ]


This passage is remarkable for the deliberately dilettante and unsystematic


**2** **"...to attain elegance. For example, the reduplication of words also has vigor in**
**some cases and charm in others, and likewise a word which is slightly changed or**
**inflected; also the frequent repetition of the same word either at beginnings or at**
**endings, emphasis by repetition of words, correspondence, connection, and progres-**
**sion; some change of meaning in the same word employed more than once, the recalling**
**of a word, and those [parts of speech] which have similar endings or cases and those**
**which are repeated in parallel form or are similar to each other. Then there is a certain**
**gradation and rounding off of periods... and antithesis... and [a word] put in many**
**cases...."** (Ill, **54, 206-7).**


34 **SOUND REPETITIONS IN ANCIENT LITERARY THEORY**


manner in which Crassus pours out his catalogue of figures. Crassus does
not elaborate on his technical terms, the identity and exact meaning of
which remain vague. On the other hand, this method enables Cicero to
give a treatise on Gorgianic figures in a nutshell.
Rhyming figures are not merely random ornaments of diction but
produce acoustic structure and rhythmical regularity:


formae vero quaedam orationis, in quibus ea concinnitas est ut
sequatur numerus necessario. nam cum aut par pari refertur aut
contrarium contrario opponitur aut quae similiter cadunt verba
verbis comparantur, quidquid ita concluditur, plerumque fit ut
numerose cadat. [3 ]


In periodic prose as well as in quantitative poetry acoustic units, or
_cola,_ are defined especially by the elaboration of beginnings and endings,
while the middle is less noticeable:


**Tcavxeq yoov iStcog xrov xe npcbxcov (ivr||iov£uo(i8v Kai xcov uaxaxwv,**

**Kai** otto **xouxcov Kivoi)(ie9a, imo 8e xcov** ixexa^i) **e^axxov <BG7tsp**

**¿YKpU7txo|isv(ov f| svacpavi^oneviov.** **[4 ]**


Although endings are more important, beginnings of _cola_ should not
be carelessly treated. According to Quintilian "next to conclusions
beginnings require the greatest care; for to them the listener likewise pays
attention". [5] For the elaboration of beginnings of _cola_ in prose and poetry
various rhyming devices are recommended. For consecutive beginnings
of _cola_ in periodic prose not only repetitions of words but also similar
inflectional forms are acceptable:


... initia hoc modo: ded _erim_ periculis omnibus, obtulmm insidiis,
obiecmm invidiae 6


An example in Aristotle shows that at beginnings of _cola_ even puns
may be used which involve slight metathesis:


**8** **"In fact there are certain patterns of speech in which the symmetry is such that**
**regular rhythm inevitably results from it. For example, whenever statements are put**
**in parallel or antithetical form or words with similar endings [cases] are matched,**
**anything concluded in this way usually turns out to have regular rhythmical cadence."**
**(Cicero,** _**Orator,**_ **65, 220).**
**4 "At any rate we all distinctly remember the first and last [syllables], and by these**
**we are stirred, but less by those in between, as if they were concealed or obliterated."**
**(Demetrius, 229v, II, 39).**
**'** **proximam clausulis diligentiam postulant initia: nam et in haec intentus auditor est.**
**(IX, 4, 62).**
**e** **"... beginnings thus; dederz'm... obtulmm... obiecer/m...." (Quintilian, IX, 3, 45).**

**36** SOUND REPETITIONS IN ANCIENT LITERARY THEORY


Beginnings of acoustic units may receive considerable attention, but
endings are far more important for the acoustic structure. This is true in
periodic prose as well as in poetry, both of which have audible breaks
at the end of acoustic units. As Aristotle remarks, these breaks are
pleasant to the listener because they divide the speech into well-proportioned sections, and they assist the memory by creating regular rhythm. [13 ]

Periodic prose, however, was spoken while poetry was sung. [14] Actual
pauses at endings of _cola_ were suitable only for the oral delivery of poetry,
while spoken prose must continue in an even flow:


**Siorrep ¿v Tcp S i a ^ s y s a S a i cpeuyonev t o i a x a v a i xfiv cpcovfiv** **¿v**


_8s_ **iff) netapSeiv xouvavxiov TtoioO|aev,** t o jisv **y a p a u v s / s q (peuyonev,**

t o **5'eaxavai xf|v cpcovfiv rog | i a ^ i a x a** S uokohev. [1 5 ]


Boethius mentions three forms of oral delivery, an intermediate degree
of continuity being suitable for epic:


omnis vox aut <juv **£%f|<;** est, quae continua, aut Siaaxr **)|xaxiKfi,** quae
dicitur cum intervallo suspensa. et continua quidem est, qua
loquentes vel prosam orationem legentes verba percurrimus
8ia<TTT|H(mKT| autem est ea, quam canendo suspendimus estque
vox ipsa tardior et per modulandas varietates quoddam faciens
intervallum his, ut Albinus autumat, additur tertia differentia,
quae medias voces possit includere, cum scilicet heroum poema
legimus neque continuo cursu, ut prosam, neque suspenso segniorique modo vocis, ut canticum. [16 ]


Similar comments on pause in oral delivery are made by Martianus
Capella, who likewise associates continuity of utterance with prose,
while he considers audible intervals as a feature of musical composition. [17 ]

**THE NATURE OF RHYMING FIGURES** **37**


It may be assumed that all the same orators did pause slightly at endings
jf acoustic units. In recitations of poetry pauses probably coincided with
verse endings and caesuras, which divided the text into regular sections.
Although metrical regularity in artistic prose was avoided, oratory did
have acoustic structure according to regular units of composition. In oral
delivery divisions between these units must be perceptible to the listener
without the help of written punctuation. [18 ]

Carefully worked out endings in oratory could function as audible
punctuation marks and thus define the end of a _**colon**_ or period. Aristotle
recommends long final syllables for the effective punctuation of sentences:


_**akXà**_ **8eï xfj jimcpâ (scil., GoI^aPfj) àTCOKÔTtxscâai Kai 5t|^** T **|** V

**elvai xf|v TeA,eutf|v, |xf| 5 i à xôv ypacpéa, |ir|8è 5ià xf|v Ttapaypatpriv,**

_**àXkà**_ **Sià** TÔV **puS^iôv.** **[19 ]**


Rhythmical cadence at endings, according to Cicero, is one of the most
important features of periodic prose :


qua re cum aures extremum semper expectent in eoque acquiesçant,
id vacare numéro non oportet, sed ad hunc exitum iam a principio
ferri debet verborum ilia comprehensio et tota a capite ita fluere,
ut ad extremum veniens ipsa consistât. [20 ]


It is taken for granted that the function of regular rhythm in prose
corresponds to that of metre in poetry:

and that the elaboration of clauses in prose was introduced after the

ut igitur poeticae versus inventus est terminatione aurium, observation prudentium, sic in oratione animadversum est, multo illud

**the scribe nor by the punctuation, but by the rhythm." (** _**Art of Rhetoric,**_ **1409a, III, 8).**
**40** **"Therefore, since the listener always waits for the end and relaxes in it, this part**
**should not be without rhythm, but towards this termination the period must be con-**
**ducted from the start, and the whole must flow from its beginning in such a way that**
**when it arrives at the end it stops by itself." (** _**Orator**_ **, 59, 199).**
**21** **"Just as poetry is divided by metre... thus the so-called** _**cola**_ **divide and punctuate**
**prose oratory, as they stop the speaker and also that which is spoken...." (Demetrius,**
**226r, I, 1).**

tamen serius, sed eadem natura admonente, esse quosdam certos
cursus conclusionesque verborum. [22 ]


Although the rhythmical elaboration of oratory might not develop into
metre, rhythmical conclusions of prose periods were comparable to those
of verse:


versus in oratione si efficitur coniunctione verborum, vitium est, et
tamen earn coniunctionem sicuti versum numerose cadere et
quadrare et perfici volumus. [23 ]


For the effective conclusion of _cola_ and periods in oratory both metrical
clauses and rhymed endings are recommended. Cicero's usual technical
term to described properly elaborated clauses, _numerose et apte cadere,_ _[2]_ _*_
probably illustrates both these acoustic aspects of periodic conclusions.
The term _numerose_ may refer to the ordered rhythmical or metrical aspect
of the clauses while _apte_ may refer to the element of matched sounds
"expected by the ear", i.e., rhyming figures. [25 ]

Both _numerose_ and _apte,_ however, may have been Cicero's Latin
equivalents for Greek technical terms. _Numerus_ may be a translation of
the Greek pu9|ioq- api%i6<; ('numbers', 'rhythm'); _aptare_ may correspond
to covSevvai icai apixocrai ('to unite and fit together'). According to
Longinus cruv9eivai and apuocrai are devices by which writers and poets
who are not sublime can achieve 'bulk' and 'interval' and not appear
insignificant:


_aXka_ |IT)v 8n ye _TioXkoi_ Kai aoyypaipecov Kai TIOITITCBV OUK _**OVXEC,**_

rcepte|3aA.ovxo. [26 ]


Longinus's reference is to formal aspects of poetry and periodic prose.
Although _oovSeivai_ and _ap/xoaai_ also have logical or mathematical

**THE NATURE OF RHYMING FIGURES** 39


connotations, in this passage they apparently refer to parallel constructions and metrical or rhyming clauses. OYKO<; and Sidcrrijia (cf.
Boethius's term, _vox_ Siaaxruumicfi, p. 36) probably are coordinated
technical terms referring to the effects of competent 'joining and fitting
together' of _cola._ 'Volume' or 'roundedness' results from parallel
constructions and 'interval' or 'pause' from the careful adaptation of
endings.
Examples of apt cadence may be found in Cicero's own works:


sed quae incisim aut membratim efferuntur, ea vel aptissime cadere
debent, ut est apud me: domus tibi deerat? at habebas. pecunia
superabat? at egebas. haec incise dicta sunt quattuor; at membratim
quae sequuntur duo: incurristi amens in columnas, in alienos
insanus insanisti. [27 ]


These examples, besides illustrating the audible punctuation and
termination of acoustic units ( _caesa_ and _membra_ respectively), also show
parallel and symmetrical rhyme patterns. The _caesa_ are arranged in
regular parallels:


domus tibi deerai
_at habebas_
pecunia superabai
_at egebas_


while the membra are arranged symmetrically:


_incurristi_ amens _in_ colum _nas/in_ alienoi insanus _insanisti_


In the last example the acoustic correspondence between the beginning
of the first member and the ending of the second: _incurristi - insanisti_
acts as a frame for the combined sound-sense symmetry of the enclosed
part; _amens_ corresponds to _insanus_ semantically and _in columnas - in_
_alienos_ share preposition and case and have some sounds in common.
Apt conclusions of acoustic units being essentially poetic devices, they
are frequently exemplified from poetry, especially from Homer:


**"YXXco £7i ix^uoevT« Kai "Ep(icp 8ivf|£vrz** **[28 ]**


**27** **"But what is expressed in the form of coordinated** _**caesa**_ **[short units] or** _**membra**_

**[longer units] must have perfectly well adapted terminations, as in my own works:**
_**domus tibi deerat**_ **here the clauses in the form of** _**caesa**_ **are four; while the following**
**in the form of** _**membra**_ **are two: incurristi** **" (** _**Orator**_ **, 67, 223-4).**
**28** **Iliad, XIII, 392; example of ¿uoiOTsXeuxov, quoted by Phoebammon,** _**schem.,**_
**p. 5 17, vol. 8, Walz.**


**40** SOUND REPETITIONS IN ANCIENT LITERARY THEORY


An example of apt terminations combined with extensive parallelism
may be found in Persius :


non ego cum scribo, _si_ forte _quid aptius exit,_
quando haec rara avis est, _si quid_ tamen _aptius exit,_
laudari metuam 29


where the repetition perhaps is meant to exemplify that which it describes,
viz., _aptius._
Horace's _Ars Poetica_ contains another example of literary criticism in
the form of apt cadence:


non satis est pulchra esse poemata: dulcia s _unto_
et quocumque volent animum auditoris agwn/o. [30 ]


In this couplet the persuasive power of euphony is suggested by the
unusual, conspicuously rhyming terminations _sunto - agunto._ Considering
that similar statements in the context of sound repetitions are made by
other authorities, one may assume that Horace's reference is not to the
charms of poetry in general but rather to the specific appeal of rhyming
figures.
Parallel and symmetrical arrangements of rhyme are combined in a
further comment by Horace on 'sweetness' in literary expression:


omne tujit punctum qui miscuit utile dulci
feciorem _delectando_ pariterque mon _endo._ _[31 ]_


In this example the similar cadences in delecta«i/o - _monendo_ are obliterated by other effects, such as symmetrical sounds in omne - monendo,
tulit - utile (dulci), and repeated syllables in /eciorem - de/ec/ando.
If parallel and symmetrical arrangements of acoustic units are to
produce rhythmical regularity, however, they must have not only
properly adapted beginnings and endings but also balanced length. In
poetry this requirement is automatically satisfied by the metrical scheme,
but in prose there are no fixed measures for acoustic units. Too neat
regularity in oratory must indeed be avoided because it would result in
poetry. Nevertheless, the similar length and shape of coordinated


**"** **"When I am writing, if anything happens to terminate more aptly, / while that is**
**a rare bird, yet if something does terminate more aptly /1 shall not be afraid of praise**
_**(Satires,**_ **I, 45-7).**
**"It is not enough for poems to be beautiful: they should be sweet / and lead the**
**listener's fancy wherever they may wish." (11. 99-100).**
**"** **"He has carried the entire vote who has mixed the useful with the sweet, / delighting**
**the reader as well as instructing him."** _**(ibid.,**_ **11. 343-4).**

Similarity of length may be either approximate, as in the figure called

has its counterpart in the other(s):

hie uno vel altero addito in quovis loco cetera excurrunt. [32 ]


Too exact balancing of _cola_ is not always recommended:


conpar appellatur quod habet in se membra orationis, de quibus
ante diximus ( K O & O V, K O | 4 I A, membrum, articulus or caesum) quae
constent ex pari fere numero syllabarum. hoc non denumeratione
nostra fiet - nam id quidem puerile est - sed tantum adferet usus
et exercitatio facultatis, ut animi quodam sensu par membrum
superiori referre possimus 33


The dissimilarity may be slight and may consist, e.g., in the lengthening

et denuntiatione potentium perterritus, neque difficultatibus et
magnitudine rei _a proposito_ deiectus. [34 ]


Similarity of _cola,_ as in the above example, often consists in similar
endings as well as comparable, overall shape and length:

In these examples rhythmical regularity results from similar length of
coordinated _**cola,**_ parallel structures, and punctuation by end rhyme.
A special method for the production of equal members is the antithetical statement, in which contrasting _**cola**_ are arranged in parallel or
symmetrical form.
It is true that antithetical statements primarily express contrasting
ideas and thus belong to the figures of thought. Aristotle even believes
that the appeal of antithesis may partly be explained by its closeness to
syllogism. [42 ]


As far as it expresses a real contrast, the antithetical statement or
àvxiSexov must be considered a figure of thought. But the formal
implications of dvxiSexov are so important that it may also be defined
as a figure of diction; as such it may be recommended for balanced
periods which are likely to result in _**numerus.**_
The various aspects of avxiSexov make it difficult to classify. It is not
surprising that Quintilian cannot determine whether one form of antithesis, the _**comparatio,**_ variously classified by other authorities, is to be
considered a figure of diction or of thought.
At any rate antithesis is not recommended for purely formal purposes
where no contrast exists at all :

44 **SOUND REPETITIONS IN ANCIENT LITERARY THEORY**


The formal parallelism of genuine avxiOexov is due to the exact
correspondence of opposite _**cola,**_ which results in _**numerus,**_ rhyme, and
even verse.
In some examples acoustic similarity is slight while logical contrasts are
stressed:


vigilas tu de nocte, ut tuis consultatoribus respondeas, ille, ut eo,
quo intendit, mature cum exercitu perveniat: te gallorum, ilium
bucinarum cantus exsuscitat: tu actionem instituis, ille aciem
instruit: tu caves ne consultatores tui, ille ne urbes aut castra
capiantur. [44 ]


As Quintilian remarks, there is more than one way to balance opposites:


contrapositum autem vel, ut quidem vocant, contentio (avxOexov
dicitur) non uno lit modo. nam et fit, si singula singulis opponuntur... "vicit pudorem libido, timorem audacia", et bina binis: "non
nostri ingenii, vestri auxilii est", et sententiae sententiis: "dominetur
in contionibus, iaceat in iudiciis." [45 ]


In other examples rhythm and rhyme effects are more obvious:
"inimicis te placabilem, amicis inexorabilem praebes", [46] which
satisfies Cicero's requirement of harmonious cadence. Cicero's own
rhyming example of balanced _**caesa**_ (cf. pp. 15 and 39) is quoted as an
example of avxOexov by Aquila [47] and Martianus Capella, who mentions
rhyme effects as typical for this figure:


avti&exov id est compositum ex contrariis cum verba pugnantia
inter se paribus vocibus colliduntur vel paria paribus opponuntur..., [48 ]


**joke by the poet Epicharmus: 'At one time I was with them, at another time, however,**
**I was at their home.' Obviously the same thing is expressed and not a contrast. The**
**form of expression which is mimicking antithesis resembles a fallacy. But probably**
**Epicharmus was joking when he used antithesis in this way and at the same time was**
**making fun of the rhetoricians." (Demetrius, I, 24).**
**44** **Quintilian, IX, 3, 32.**
**46** **"Arrangement by opposites, or contrast, as some authors call it (the reference is**
**to dviiSexov), is not achieved by one pattern only. It results when single words are**
**contrasted with each other... and pairs of words with pairs... and sentences with**
**sentences...." (IX, 3, 81).**
_**48**_ _**Auctor ad Herennium,**_ **IV, 15, 21.**
**47** **§22.**
**48** **"dviiSexov is an aggregate of opposites, when contrasting words are forced**
**together by similar sounds or similar parts of speech are opposed to each other** **"**
**(174G, V, 531).**


THE NATURE OF RHYMING FIGURES **45**


One may therefore say that dvxi&exov apart from its logical implications is a formal arrangement conducive to _numerus._ This is openly
admitted by Cicero, who recommends antithetical balance for the express
purpose of decoration, and who in the acoustic elaboration of his own
examples far exceeds the parallelism necessary to formulate a logical
antithesis:


semper haec, quae Graeci avxiSexa nominant, cum contrariis
opponuntur contraria, numerum oratorium necessitate ipsa
efficiunt etiam sine industria nos etiam in hoc genere frequentes,
ut ilia sunt in quarta accusationis: conferte hanc pacem cum illo
bello, huius praetoris adventum cum illius imperatoris victoria,
huius cohortem impuram cum illius exercitu invicto, huius libidines
cum illius continentia: ab illo qui cepit conditas, ab hoc qui
constitutas accepit captas dicetis Syracusas. [49 ]


It is obvious that Cicero in this example, besides working in some
jingles like _illo - bello, praetoris - imperatoris - victoria,_ etymological
figures like _cepit - accepit - captas,_ and symmetrical balance of short and
long members, makes the most of his opportunities to create rhyme.
Beginnings and endings of clauses are heavily paralleled and appear to
be deliberately rhymed, although Cicero claims that the harmonious
effect of antithesis, like that of parallelism, is automatic:


sive paribus paria redduntur, sive opponuntur contraria, suapte
natura numerosa sunt, etiam si nihil est factum de industria. [50 ]


According to Cicero antithesis does not only result in prose rhythm
but also in verse:


haec enim talia sunt, ut, quia referuntur eo quo debent referri,
intellegamus non quaesitum esse numerum, sed secutum. quod fit
item in contrariis referendis, ut ilia sunt quibus non modo numerosa
oratio sed etiam versus efficitur:
earn quam nihil accusas damnas —
condemnas dixisset qui versum effugere vellet —
bene quam meritam esse autumas (dicis)


49 "Those arrangements which the Greeks call dvtiSsxa, where opposites are contrasted against each other, always naturally result in regular prose rhythm, even without
effort We, too, frequently speak in this manner, as, for example, the well-known
passage in the fourth indictment (against Verres) " ( _Orator_, 50, 166-7).
60 "If similar parts are repeated or opposites are contrasted, they have regular rhythm
according to their own nature, even if nothing is done on purpose." _(ibid.,_ 49, 164).


**46** SOUND REPETITIONS IN ANCIENT LITERARY THEORY


male merere? id quod scis prodest nihil; id quod nescis obest?
versum efficit ipsa relatio contrariorum. idem esset in oratione
numerosum: quod scis nihil prodest: quod nescis multum obest. [51 ]


Cicero's example might be suspected of corruption because it is not
consistent with the general argument. The example is said to illustrate
antithesis resulting in verse patterns; one who "wishes to avoid verse"
accordingly should avoid that which produces the verse, i.e., antithesis.
The proposed substitution in the text of _condemnas_ for _damnas,_ however,
is merely a synonym and does not affect the antithesis.
Although the last part of the example contains neatly balanced
opposites in verse


... id quod scis prodest nihil; id quod nescis obest?


the argument is spoiled by the addition of a nearly identical example in
prose: "quod scis nihil prodest; quod nescis multum obest."
What is avoided in the prose example is not antithesis but metre.
Cicero's example does not demonstrate specifically how antithetical
balance may effect a verse, since the same antithesis is also shown to
effect prose rhythm.
On the other hand, Cicero treats antithesis, which may result in prose
_numerus,_ as a device deliberately used in poetry. avxiSexov with its
implications of acoustic balance and rhyme thus appears as a figure which
is neither prosaic in itself nor incompatible with poetry.
Symmetry from dvxOexov results if one of the members of an antithetical statement is placed in reverse order, which usually implies changes
of inflection. There are however symmetrical arrangements without
changes of inflection, as in Quintilian's example of _distinctio: "od.it_
populus Romanus _privatam luxuriant, publicam magnificentiam_ _diligit,"_ _[52 ]_

Figures based on symmetry often are listed under the head of avxiSsxov,
although they may be further classified according to special features. The
degree of symmetry varies from the moderate forms suitable for forensic
oratory to the highly polished examples from proverbs and poetry.


contentio est cum ex contrariis rebus oratio conficitur, hoc pacto:


**61** **"In fact these are such, because they are balanced where they must be balanced,**
**that we understand that prose rhythm was not contrived but followed by itself. The**
**same happens in the balancing of contrasts, as, for example, those which not only**
**result in prose rhythm but even in verse** **If one had wished to avoid verse he would**
**have said** _**condemnas**_ **a verse results from the very balance of opposites. The same**
**would be regular rhythm in prose** **"** _**(ibid.,**_ **49, 165-6).**
**"** **IX, 3, 82.**

or in the following pentameter line with Leonine rhyme:

In these examples the organizing effect of antithesis is combined with the
stable balance of symmetry. Inverse word order in the second, contrasted
_colon_ accounts for the symmetry while rhyme effects are mostly due to
formal parallelism. The formal balance of logical opposites involves
syntactic and grammatical parallelism of contrasted members and thus
may produce rhyme.

After giving an example of Tidpicov, Alexander remarks that it also

**si 8s to 7tapd5eiyna touto Kai ohoiots^eutov ¿cttiv, ouSev**

**Siatpspsi. n o / A o i yap Xoyoi Kai ¿k 5uo Kai ek tcXeiovcov CTxruidxcov**

**CTuyKEixai.** **[58 ]**


Statements of this kind may have led to the modern definition of
'automatic rhyme', i.e., of rhyme as an unintentional side effect of
rhetorical balance. One must bear in mind, however, that parallel and
antithetical _cola_ are themselves figures and are recommended for prose
and poetry precisely because, among other effects, they produce rhyme.
**¿hoioteXeotov** therefore may be a cause of ndpioov as well as an effect.
On the other hand, rhymes and rhyme-like effects at endings and
beginnings of _cola_ may result from figures which are not based on
grammatical coincidence. If in these figures rhyme occurs in parallel
positions it may not be explained as a concomitant of syntactic parallelism
but must be considered as the result of deliberate arrangement.
An example of similar endings of _cola_ without syntactic parallelism is
Ovid's Leonine pentameter line (quoted by Quintilian as an example of
name-calling because it is a pun on a proper name):


cur ego non _dicam_ Furia, te furiam? [59 ]


In this example the metrically stressed last syllables of both hemistichs are
identical although one belongs to a verb and the other to a noun, and no
syntactic parallelism is involved.


Some figures not only are not connected with syntactic or grammatical
similarity but, on the contrary, consist of several inflectional forms of the
same word (jtifficnq) or of several derivatives from the same root
('productive' forms). From the examples collected in ancient catalogues,
as well as from the frequency of these effects in classical Greek and Latin


**"** **"At this point anyone may see for himself that several figures of diction often**
**coincide. For if one says, for example:** **he has combined three figures in one**
**expression, similar** _**cola,**_ **similar cases, and disjunction. The same may also occur in**
**other figures." (Aquila, § 47).**
**68** **"But if this example at the same time is a** _**homoeoteleuton,**_ **it makes no difference;**
**in fact many expressions consist of two or more figures." (** _**schem. lex.,**_ **p. 485, vol. 8,**
**Walz).**

THE NATURE OF RHYMING FIGURES **49**


poetry, it is obvious that these varieties of figures were used to emphasize
endings and beginnings of acoustic units.
A special group of rhyming figures are the puns, which include elements
of thought (jcapovonaaia). These figures consist in the repetition of
words in identical or slightly altered form, combined with some change
of meaning. While other rhyming devices are effective in metrically and
acoustically important positions, puns and other sound-sense combinations may be considered less so, because part of their appeal is intellectual
and thus independent of the acoustic structure. Puns all the same
occasionally form rhymes at endings or beginnings of _cola._ But unlike
other rhyming figures puns never occur in sequences of repeated rhymes,
probably because a pun does not improve with repetition.
One may add that puns and plays upon words in the place of end rhyme
are used in rhymed post-classical poetry. Sound-sense figures at verse
endings can be found in Chaucer:


The hooly blisful martir for to _seke,_
That hem hath holpen whan that they were _seeke._ _[60 ]_


Extreme forms occur in Petrarca's sonnets, where end rhyme patterns
may consist of several repetitions of a word in various meanings, for
example:


(a) parte
(b) luce
(b) luce
(a) parte
(a) parte
(b) luce
(b) luce
(a) parte etc. [61 ]


It is true that Petrarca's conceits are based on acoustically identical
repetition rather than slightly changed words, and thus are formally
similar to conventional rhymes in post-classical poetry.


_,0_ _The Canterbury Tales,_ General Prologue, 17-8.
_1,1 In Vita di Madonna Laura,_ XVIII.


**IV**


_VITIUM_ AND RHYMING FIGURES


**totidem autem generibus corrumpitur**
**oratio, quot ornatur.**
**(Quintilian)**


Rhyming figures are close to jingles, i.e., to literary faults, and even may
themselves result in _vitium._ Whether a specific form of sound repetition
is to be considered a figure or a fault depends less on its own nature than
on context and circumstances.
Generally poets may get away with — and even be praised for — sound
effects which would be intolerable in a serious speaker. But repetitions
of sound in poetry as well as prose may be criticized if they disturb the
rhythm or result in monotony.
These being to some extent matters of taste, it is hard to define exactly
under what circumstances sound repetition constitutes _vitium._ It seems
safe to say, however, that sound repetitions are objectionable to ancient
critics if they fail in their natural function of creating _numerus,_ i.e., if their
effect is unrelated or contrary to the rhythmical or metrical structure of
the whole.
Although _vitium_ often results from sound repetitions which are not
figures, e.g., consonant clusters, colliding vowels, and compound clusters,
it may also result from actual figures, such as reduplication of words,
derivations from similar roots, similar cases, and similar endings.
The definition of _vitium_ is complicated by its closeness to literary
virtues [1] which results in confusion of literary taste:


hoc autem adeo verum est, ut, cum in hac maxime parte [scil.,
ornatus] sint vicina virtutibus vitia, etiam qui vitiis utuntur virtutum
tamen iis nomen imponant. [2 ]


**1** **Quintilian, VIII, 3, 58.**
**2** **"This is so true that, since in this matter** _[viz.,_ **decoration] faults are close to literary**
**virtues, even those who use faulty devices call them virtues nevertheless."** _(ibid.,_ **VIII,**
**3,7).**


_**VITIUM**_ **AND RHYMING FIGURES** 51


... excusantur haec vitia [scil., barbarismus _and_ soloecismus] aut
consuetudine aut auctoritate aut vetustate aut denique vicinitate
virtutum (nam saepe a figuris ea separare difficile est) 3


The situation is further complicated by the different standards of
poetry and prose. Forms of diction which constitute _vitium_ in oratory
are not necessarily faults in poetry. It is therefore possible to find _vitium_
for orators exemplified by verses from established poetry, such as the
_Annals_ of Ennius. [4] Conversely, forms that would be strange or incorrect
in prose may be considered figures in poetry:


... grammaticus deprendat quae barbara, quae impropria, quae
contra legem loquendi sint posita, non ut ex his utique inprobentur
poetae (quibus, quia plerumque servire metro coguntur, adeo
ignoscitur, ut vitia ipsa aliis in carmine appellationibus nominentur:
HETa7t^acj|ioi)g enim / et axrinaTianoix; / et oxfiixata, ut dixi,
vocamus et laudem virtutis necessitati damus ) [5 ]


While orators had to limit themselves to standard usage poets were
allowed to introduce changes and innovations of diction. These forms
sometimes became generally accepted:


quas novas verbi declinationes ratione introductas respuet forum,
his boni poetae, maxime scenici, consuetudine subigere aures populi
debent, quod poetae multum possunt in hoc verba perperam
dicta apud antiquos aliquos propter poetas non modo nunc
dicuntur recte, sed etiam quae ratione dicta sunt turn, nunc perperam
dicuntur. [6 ]


The greater freedom of poets was evident, e.g., in the use of analogy:


**8** **"These faults** _**[viz.,**_ **barbarism and solecism] are excused on the strength of usage**
**or authority or antiquity, or indeed because they are close to literary virtues (it certainly**
**is often difficult to distinguish them from figures of speech)...."** _**{ibid.,**_ **I, 5, 5).**
_**4**_ _**Auctor ad Herennium,**_ **IV, 12, 18.**
**6** **"The teacher should catch barbarisms, unidiomatic expressions, and irregular**
**forms; not that poets were by any means blamed for them (since poets generally must**
**observe the metre they have so much license that even actual faults are called by**
**different names in poetry: as I mentioned before, we call them 'transformations', for**
**example, / and 'figurative forms' / and 'figures' and give the praise of virtue to neces-**
**sity....)". (Quintilian, I, 8, 13-4).**
**'** **"New modifications of a word introduced according to rule are rejected by the**
**public; to such forms the poets, especially the dramatists, by constant usage must**
**train the taste of the audience, since poets have great influence in this respect** **Thanks**
**to the poets not only words which were said incorrectly by some ancients now are said**
**in the right form, but words which formerly were said according to rule now are said**
**incorrectly." (Varro,** _**De Lingua Latina,**_ **IX, 17).**


**52** SOUND REPETITIONS IN ANCIENT LITERARY THEORY


... cum orator non debeat in omnibus uti, quod sine offensione non
potest facere, cum poeta transilire lineas impune possit. [7 ]


nam liberius potest poeta quam orator sequi analogias. [8 ]


Poetic license went so far that even the strange sound effects resulting
from Ennius's reduplication of a barbarism (possibly in imitation of
Homeric forms, such as %oA.oio - (ieya^oio - cpovoio?) might be excused
in poetry:


at in eadem vitii geminatione "Metioeo Fufetioeo" dicens Ennius
poetico iure defenditur. [9 ]


On the other hand even poets may be criticized for sound effects which
disturb or destroy the rhythm of the whole. Such effects result from
unsystematic sound repetitions which necessitate breaks where continuity
is expected or drown out the rhythm with their own monotonous beat.
Random repetitions of sound may cause faulty combinations:


conpositio est verborum constructio quae facit omnes partes orationis
aequaliter perpolitas. ea conservabitur si fugiemus crebras vocalium
concursiones, quae vastam atque hiantem orationem reddunt, ut
haec est: 'baccre _aeneae_ amoenissime z'npendebant'; et si vitabimus
eiusdem litterae nimiam adsiduitatem, cui vitio versus hie erit
exemplo:

    - _Tite, lute, Taii,_ fibi _tanta, t_ yranne, iulis/i et hie eiusdem poetae
(Ennius):
_quoiquam_ _quicquam_ _quemquam,_ _quemque_ _qui&que_ conveniat,
neget. [10 ]


The attractive style in oratory must be free of faulty combinations:


suave autem genus erit dicendi primum elegantia et iucunditate


**'** **"...since the orator may not use all forms [of analogy]; he cannot do so without**
**giving offense, whereas the poet may cross the lines with impunity."** _{ibid.,_ **IX, 5).**
**8** **"The poet certainly may seek analogies more freely than the orator."** _(ibid.,_ **IX, 115)**
**9** **"But concerning the same reduplication of a fault** _[viz.,_ **barbarism] Ennius's ex-**
**pression, '** _Metioeo Fufetioeo',_ **is justified on the strength of poetic license." (Quintilian,**
**I, 5, 12).**
**10** **"Proper disposition is an arrangement of words which gives an even finish to all**
**parts of the speech. It will be maintained if we shun frequent collisions of vowels,**
**which make the speech empty and gaping, as in the following example** **and if we**
**avoid the unremitting recurrence of the same letter, a fault for which the following**
**verse may serve as an example** **and this line by the same poet** **"** _(Auctor_ _ad_
_Herennium,_ **IV, 12, 18).**


_**VITIUM**_ **AND RHYMING FIGURES** **53**


verborum sonantium et levium: deinde coniunctione, quae neque
asperos habeat concursus neque diiunctos atque hiantis 11


Random reduplications and harsh collisions between the final letter of
a word and the first letter of the following word must be avoided. [12] In
some cases awkward clusters and reduplications might be prevented by
omission of the final letter:


ceterum consonantes quoque, earumque praecipue, quae sunt
asperiores, in commissura verborum rixantur, ut s ultima cum x
proxima, quarum tristior etiam, si binae collidantur, stridor est,
ut 'a _rs_ iiudiorum'. quae fuit causa et Servio Sulpicio subtrahendae
s litterae, quotiens ultima esset aliaque consonante susciperetur 13


Faulty sound repetitions are classified according to the letters or
effects involved:


**. . . vitia oris et linguae: io)Taiacj|iou<; / et (iuxaKio|ioi)g et XapSa-**

**KiCTHouq /** **14**


... vitium maximum est hiulcas et asperas, frenos etiam, iotacismos
mytacismos labdacismos homoeoprophora dysprophora et polysigma non vitare vel cuiuslibet litterae assiduitatem in odium
repetitam 15


Reduplications and clusters of consonants break the continuity and
impair the rhythm in prose and poetry. Martianus Capella adds the
remark that harsh combinations between the penultimate and last words
must be avoided:


**11** **"Now the style of oratory will be pleasant in the first place through the elegance**
**and cheerfulness of sonorous and smooth diction; also through proper connection,**
**which should have neither harsh collisions nor abrupt and yawning gaps** **" (Cicero,**
_**Partitiones Oratoriae,**_ **6, 21).**
**12** **Cicero,** _**De Oratore,**_ **III, 43, 171-2;** _**Orator,**_ **44, 149;** _**Partitiones Oratoriae,**_ **6, 21;**
**Quintilian, IX, 4, 37.**
**13** **"Besides, even consonants, especially those which are somewhat harsh, disagree at**
**the juncture of words, as final** _**s**_ **with following** _**x.**_ **This is even worse if double con-**
**sonants collide; there is a hissing sound, as in 'a** _**rs**_ **i/udiorum'. For the same reason**
**Servius Sulpicius thought that the letter** _**s**_ **ought to be omitted whenever it is the final**

**14** **"...faults of speech and language: too many i's /and m's and l's/** **"** _**(ibid.,**_ **I, 5,**
**32).**
**16** **"(In composition) it is a very grave fault not to avoid gaps and harsh combinations,**
**as well as obstructions, too many i's, m's, l's, alliterations, tongue-twisters, and**
**cumulations of s's, in short, the unremitting frequency of any letter repeated to the**
**point of disgust...." (Martianus Capella, 167G, V, 514).**

labdacismus ubi 1 plurimum dissonat ut si dicas: "sol et luna luce

iure irascitur" [note: consonantal and vocalic _**iotas**_ are combined],
polysigmaubi s littera crebrius geminatur: "Sosia in solario soleas
sarciebat suas". homoeoprophoron est cum dicitur "o Tite, tute,
Tati, tibi tanta, tyranne, tulisti". dysprophoron ut si quis dicat
"persuasitrices praestigiatrices atque inductrices strigae". asperae
inter paenultimum ultimumque verbum maxime vitandae, cuius
exemplum est si dicas "phaleras ablatas gratis", aut si iuret auriga
"per lora per flagella per frena". [16 ]


Martianus's last rule perhaps may be compared to Porson's law and
other rules on overtaxing metrically weak long positions at endings of
_**cola.**_ Porson found that in the iambic trimeter of classical Greek lyrics
and tragedy a long final _**anceps**_ is not at the same time the last syllable of
a polysyllabic word. One can probably explain this rule (and comparable
rules for other metres) by the natural pause which follows after the
pronunciation of a polysyllabic word. Although such a pause would be
slight, it might noticeably add to the time already required to pronounce
the long final syllable. Thus the _**anceps,**_ where a long syllable is already
squeezed into the space of a normally short one, would require even more
time for pronunciation, and the general rhythm would be disturbed.
The addition of slight pauses to long _**ancipites**_ apparently was tolerated
in the rest of the verse but avoided at its conclusion. Porson's law thus
tallies with the ancient principle that at endings of _**cola**_ rhythmical or
metrical regularity must be strictly observed. Martianus Capella emphasizes the same principle by ruling out time-consuming obstructions at
endings of _**cola.**_
A line better than "phaleras ablatas gratis" may be found in Martianus's
own poetry: "phaleras parat libello". [17] This line has no harsh combinations between the two last words.

_**VITIUM**_ **AND RHYMING FIGURES** 55


The only purpose for which consonant cumulation seems to be useful
is comic effects, as in Cicero's example:


... tota Tarracina turn omnibus in parietibus inscriptas fuisse litteras
L.L.L.M.M.; cum quaereres id quid esset, senem tibi quendam
oppidanum dixisse: "lacerat lacertum Largi mordax Memmius". [18 ]


While cumulation of consonants causes excessive crowdedness,
frequency and collision of identical vowels produce 'yawning' gaps which
likewise interfere with the flow of diction. All the same collision of vowels
is not considered a _vitium_ by all authorities. [19 ]


hiulcae sunt cum in ea parte, quam diximus [scil., inter paenultimum
ultimumque verbum?] similes vocales ac similiter longae conlisam
hiantemque structuram faciunt ut si quis dicat "suscepisse se liberos
secundo omine", et ut Tullius pro Milone ait "auctoritate publica
armare", quod quidem artem dissimulans plerumque appetit
volúntate. [20 ]


According to Quintilian the collision of long, open vowels produces the
worst gaps:


...turn vocalium concursus: quod cum accidit, hiat et intersistit
et quasi laborat oratio. pessime longae, quae easdem inter se
litteras committunt, sonabunt: praecipuus tamen erit hiatus earum,
quae cavo aut patulo máxime ore efferuntur 21


while short, closed vowels are less offensive.
Collisions and reduplications of vowels, however, may be tolerated in
certain cases. Poets like Naevius and Ennius have collision of different
vowels in their verses [22], and the amplifying effect of _hiatus_ and _hiulca_
sometimes is agreeable in prose. [23 ]

On the other hand, random reduplications of compounds of vowels
and consonants ( _syllabae_ : not in the sense of 'syllable' but rather of
'compound') should be avoided:


videndum etiam, ne syllaba verbi prioris ultima et prima sequentis
consonet: quod ne quis praecipi miretur, Ciceroni in epistulis
excidit: "res mihi /in _**visae/ visae**_ sunt, Brute", et in carmine:

    - _**iortunatam**_ _**natarn**_ me consule Romam. [2 5 ]


The last example is also ridiculed by Juvenal, according to whom Cicero
would have had nothing to fear from Anthony if this had been his usual
style. [26 ]

Quintilian's examples suggest that the Leonine rhyme in fortunatam Romam is not included in his criticism. The clumsy and metrically
unsystematic reduplication of the compound, _**-natam - natam,**_ comparable
to _**-visae - visae,**_ alone seems to be condemned. Metrically unsystematic
'internal rhymes' in quantitative poetry thus would constitute _**vitium**_
for the same reason Quintilian cites in another context:


esset enim omne eiusmodi schema vitium, si non peteretur, sed
accideret. [27 ]


Thus, with exceptions, random clusters and cumulations of sound in
classical quantitative poetry must be considered as _**vitium.**_ But are not

_VITIUM_ AND RHYMING FIGURES 57


alliteration and assonance poetic devices? Although it may be argued
that, on the strength of poetic license, unsystematic sound repetitions
in poetry need not constitute _vitium_ and even might result in onomatopoetic effects, the fact remains that random repetitions of sound in ancient
literary theory are defended rather than recommended and never are
included among the downright devices of decoration.
While the aesthetic value of metrically and rhythmically effective sound
repetitions is well documented in ancient literary criticism, there is little
evidence for the aesthetic value of 'alliteration' and 'assonance' in the
modern sense. This lack of evidence suggests that such effects in quantitative poetry must be appreciated with caution.
It may be too great a simplification, for example, if the 'pattern' of
sound in a classical poem is reduced to the one principle of repetition, as
is done in R. Deutsch's study of _De Rerum Natura (The Pattern of Sound_
_in Lucretius)._ _[26 ]_

Besides actual figures, such as _homoeoteleuton,_ repetition of words in
corresponding metrical positions, and parallel constructions, Miss
Deutsch also commends examples of sound repetition which are not
figures, in particular 'alliteration' and 'assonance'. Some examples of
alliteration and assonance quoted by Miss Deutsch are:


II 116 multa minuta modis multis per inane videbis
V 545 us£?ue adeo magni refert guid guaegue gueat res
IV 229 cernere odorari licet et sentire sonare
IV 606 íaepe jolet scintilla juoí _se_ spargere in ignis. [29 ]


(Letters are italicized in original text).
These repetitions are considered as formal acoustic devices in their
own right, rather than "adaptations of sound to sense":


As is evident from the illustrations of alliteration and assonance
above, it is only rarely that in such verses this type of repetition
produces an onomatopoetic effect. [30 ]


On the contrary Lucretius's alliterations and assonances are seen as part
of an overall, deliberate system of composition which is based on sound
repetition at every level:


A detailed analysis of the many component parts of the soundscheme of Lucretius has shown that each of the separate factors is

_28_ _Bryn Mawr,_ **Dissertation, 1939.**
_29 ibid.,_ **p. 11.**
_ibid.,_ **pp. 14-5.**


**58** SOUND REPETITIONS IN ANCIENT LITERARY THEORY


based upon repetition. The repetition of similar sound in various
forms and at varying intervals is the major underlying principle by
which the many sound-devices are combined into a unified system.
The tonal pattern is unlimited in scope. It forms a broad scale
which proceeds from the smallest element to the greatest, from the
recurrence of single consonants and vowels, or alliteration and
assonance, to the repetition of long passages. [31 ]


It may be argued that this interpretation of Lucretius's sound effects
does not tally with the statements of ancient literary critics. Lucretius
is not among the standard authors mentioned in ancient literary theory
either as models of style or as warning examples. But it is possible that
some of his clusters and cumulations of sound, like those of Ennius, were
felt to be obtrusive and monotonous. Ancient critics therefore might have
considered Lucretius's 'alliterations' and 'assonances' clumsy rather than
expressive and classified these effects as _**vitium.**_
In poetry and prose, however, not only random repetitions of sound
but also regular rhyming figures may produce _**vitium**_ if overdone, because
excessive decoration creates boredom and surfeit. As Cicero expresses it,


atque eo citius in oratoris aut in poetae cincinnis ac fuco offenditur,
quod sensus in nimia voluptate natura, non mente satiantur; in
scriptis et in dictis non aurium solum, sed animi iudicio etiam magis
infucata vitia noscuntur. Qua re 'bene et praeclare' quamvis nobis
saepe dicatur; 'belle et festive' nimium saepe nolo 32


Excessive and monotonous piling up of decorations is the mark of an
unskilled speaker:


peior hac [scil., raoToXoyia] 6(aoei8sia; quae nulla varietatis gratia
levat taedium atque est tota coloris unius. qua maxime deprenditur
carens arte orator, eaque et in sententiis et in figuris et in compositione longe non animis solum, sed etiam auribus est ingratissima. [33 ]


_**81**_ _**ibid.,**_ **p. 172.**
