_VITIUM_ AND RHYMING FIGURES **59**


The point at which a figure of diction turns into a _**vitium**_ must be
defined in general terms of literary judgment and good taste. There is no
sign by which _**vitium**_ may be recognized except that it offends one's sense
of proportion:


nam prima virtus est vitio carere. igitur ante omnia ne speremus
ornatam orationem fore, quae probabilis non erit. probabile autem
Cicero id genus dicit, quod non nimis est comptum: non quia comi
expolirique non debeat (nam et haec ornatus pars est), sed quia
vitium est ubique quod nimium est. [34 ]


atque, ut semel finiam, verbum omne, quod neque intellectum
adiuvat neque ornatum, vitiosum dici potest. [35 ]


From the examples quoted in ancient criticism it is evident that rhyming
figures are most unwelcome when they follow each other too closely,
because then they create a new, short, monotonous beat which supersedes
the general rhythm of the verse or period.
This effect may result from reduplication of words, derivations from
similar roots, as well as from similar cases and endings :

vitata, interim vitium videri potest, in quod saepe incidit etiam

et in eadem desinentia, ut 'fortissimorum proximorum fidelissimorumque sociorum' in eodem vitio habentur. [37 ]


The same varieties of _vitium_ also occur in poetry:


_[vitium_ may be avoided] ... si eiusdem verbi adsiduitatem nimiam

nam cuius rationis ratio non exstet ei

flentes, plorantes, lacrimantes, obtestantes. [38 ]


Accidental forms of similar endings should not be permitted to remain

...ne homoeoptota, ne homoeoteleuta...multa iungantur inter
nomina aut pronomina in eosdem casus cadentia nomen diversi
casus interveniat. [39 ]


Thus even those sound repetitions which are recommended as literary

ilia quoque vitia sunt eiusdem loci, si cadentia similiter et desinentia
et eodem modo declinata multa iunguntur. ne verba quidem verbis
aut nomina nominibus similiaque his continuari decet, cum virtutes
etiam ipsae taedium parant nisi gratia varietatis adiutae. [40 ]

**V**


THE USE OF RHYMING FIGURES


**eiusmodi autem studia ad delectationem**
**quam ad veritatem videntur accommodatiora.**
**(Auctor ad Herennium)**


Except for the general rule that figures of any kind should never be
obtrusive or monotonous, ancient literary theorists do not limit or
regulate the use of rhyme in poetry. Poets apparently may employ
rhyme, like other devices of decoration, to the full extent of its possibilities. The poetic character of rhyming figures, on the other hand, makes
it necessary to regulate their use in prose.
Figures of sound repetition have aesthetic rather than practical or
moral implications. They are lively, neat, and decorative, but their
prettiness is the product of art. Because of their artistic quality, rhyming
figures are most suitable for epideictic oratory, which has no practical
purpose and is intended mainly for elegant display. In actual causes,
where a speaker above all must appear natural and truthful, rhyming
figures are far less appropriate.
The same standards of _**honestus ornatus**_ are not applicable to all prose

_**genres:**_


... atque ut a prima divisione ordiar, non idem demonstrativis et
deliberativis et iudicialibus causis convenit. [1 ]


adhibenda est igitur numerosa oratio, si aut laudandum est aliquid
ornatius,... ut in senatu de consulatu meo, aut exponenda narratio,
quae plus dignitatis desiderat quam doloris 2


**1** **"... and to start with the first division, the same is not appropriate for epideictic,**
**deliberative, and judiciary addresses."** _**(ibid.,**_ **VIII, 3, 11).**
**2 "Therefore prose rhythm is applicable, either if something must be praised in a more**
**distinguished form, ... as the speech in the Senate about my consulship, or if an ex-**
**pository narrative is required, which calls for dignity rather than indignation** **"**
**(Cicero,** _**Orator,**_ **62, 210).**


**62** SOUND REPETITIONS IN ANCIENT LITERARY THEORY


The purely artistic character of epideictic oratory makes moderation
in the use of figures unnecessary. Demonstrative speeches are expected,
on the contrary, to be showpieces of rhetorical splendor:


... et quoniam in his causis omnis ratio fere ad voluptatem auditoris
et ad delectationem refertur, utendum erit in eis /ornata/ oratione
et ipsa constructione verborum, ut paria paribus et similia similibus
saepe referantur, ut contraria, ut geminata, ut circumscripta
numerose, non ad similitudinem versuum, sed ad explendum
aurium sensum apto quodam quasi verborum modo. [3 ]


namque illud genus ostentationi compositum solam petit audientium
voluptatem. ideoque omnes dicendi artes aperit ornatumque
orationis exponit, ut quod non insidietur nec ad victoriam, sed ad
solum finem laudis et gloriae tendat. quare quidquid erit sententiis
populare, verbis nitidum, figuris iocundum, translationibus magnificum, compositione elaboratum, velut institor quidam eloquentiae
intuendum et paene pertractandum dabit: nam eventus ad se
ipsum, non ad causam refertur. [4 ]


These comments show that epideictic oratory, although a prose _genre,_
is almost as far removed from practical reality as poetry. Legal and
political speeches, which serve practical purposes, must be more prosaic
in form and above all may not contain too many figures. This rule
includes rhyme effects, which may create admiration but also may bring
ridicule and even raise doubts about the speaker's honesty.


demonstrativum genus omne fusiores habet liberioresque numeros,
iudiciale et contionale ut materia varium est, sic etiam ipsa conlocatione verborum. [5 ]


*** "... and since in these speeches the main concern is for the pleasure of the audience**
**and for entertainment, one must use /distinguished/ diction in them** **and even**
**structure of diction, so that parallels and similar parts of speech should often be**
**coordinated, as well as antitheses, reduplications, and rhythmical periods, not to**
**resemble poetry but to satisfy the sense of hearing, as it were, by some proper form of**
**speech." (Cicero,** _**Partitiones Oratoriae,**_ **21, 72).**
**4** **"And in fact this** _**genre,**_ **which is composed for display, only aims at entertaining**
**the audience. Therefore it unfolds all the artifices of rhetoric and exhibits the distinc-**
**tions of diction, since it has no insidious purpose and is not intended for winning a case,**
**but purely to bring praise and glory. Accordingly it offers whatever is popular in**
**meaning, brilliant in diction, pleasant in figures, magnificent in metaphors, artistic in**
**composition, like some salesman of rhetoric, for inspection and almost for handling:**
**for the result is an end in itself and not related to a cause." (Quintilian, VIII, 3, 11-2).**
**6** **"The epideictic** _**genre**_ **as a whole has more flowing and unrestricted rhythm; the**
**judicial and the political are as different from it in subject matter as they are in the very**
**arrangement of words."** _**(ibid.,**_ **IX, 4, 130).**


**THE USE OF RHYMING FIGURES** **63**


All sources agree that in actual cases rhyming figures must be used
with caution. [6 ]


...non modo non frequenter verum etiam raro in veris causis aut
forensibus circumscripte numeroseque dicendum est 7


Rhyming figures are not recommended for history, which also is a
serious prose _**genre:**_


...et historiae, quae currere debet ac ferri, minus convenissent
insistentes clausulae et debita actionibus respiratio et cludendi
inchoandique sententias ratio, in contionibus quidem etiam similiter cadentia quaedam et contraposita deprehendas. [8 ]


It is generally held that rhyming figures are most effective if used
according to their specific character, but the ancient authorities do not
completely agree on all points. Balanced sounds usually are called gay
and pretty, but liveliness and vigor are also associated with some figures,
especially those containing elements of logic.


in his quattuor generibus exornationum [scil., repetitio, conversio,
complexio, traductio] quae adhuc propositae sunt non inopia
verborum fit ut ad idem verbum redeatur saepius; sed inest festivitas, quae facilius auribus diiudicari quam verbis demonstrari
potest. [9 ]

from Athens to Stagira because of the great king, and from Stagira to Athens because

11 "Indeed those figures which are said to consist of many cases, the compounds,

12 "But these two figures, the former and this latter one, of which we have spoken

[viz., repetition with change of connotation and repetition for emphasis], are not as
effective for the structure and decoration of the speech as are those mentioned above

[viz., antithesis, balanced _cola,_ parallel _cola,_ similar cases, similar endings, and puns),
but rather for firing the battle of discussion; this is even more true of most of the
following _[viz.,_ repetition of several words, repetition of the last word of a _colon_ at the
beginning of the following _colon,_ the same word at beginning and end of one _colon,_
repetition at beginnings of _cola,_ repetition at endings of _cola,_ parallel beginnings and
endings of _cola,_ repetition in many cases, ...progression, etc.] For the same reason
Aristotle also feels that this repetition of verbs and nouns, as well as cumulation and
every trope of this type, is more suitable for public addresses and contests than for
written composition: He discusses this matter in the third book of his Rhetorical
Theory." (Aquila, § 30). _Notes:_ (1) Aquila's technical terms are interpreted and translated according to his own definitions and examples as they appear in Halm's edition
_(Rhetores Latini Minores)._ Different interpretations taking account of possible corruptions will be discussed later. (2) The reference to Aristotle is perhaps to _Art of Rhetoric,_
1413b, III, 12, 2: "juxtapositions and the frequent repetition of the same word in
written composition are rightly condemned, but in public address even rhetoricians use
them, because they are suitable for oral delivery."
la "Likewise, words that are echoed, repeated, or compounded have an amplifying
effect, and those which gradually rise from lower to higher—" (Cicero, _Partitiones_
_Oratoriae,_ 15, 54).


THE USE OF RHYMING FIGURES **65**


These comments show that in rhetorical theory definite aesthetic values
and practical uses were associated with figures of sound repetition. As
Robert P. Sonkowsky remarks,


...in the Aristotelian tradition, which includes the Theophrastan
and the Ciceronian, the techniques of delivery are not merely
something that is added in a superficial way after the process of
literary composition has been completed, but something that is
vitally involved in the very labors of composition anticipating the
public presentation. [14 ]


The competent use of rhyming figures in actual causes may produce
admirable results:


omne genus orationis, et grave et mediocre et attenuatum, dignitate
adficiunt exornationes, quae si rarae disponentur, distinctam sicuti
coloribus... reddunt orationem. [15 ]


verum docet nos et Demosthenes et Cicero earn optime orationem
esse compositam, quae cum ilia ornamenta parium et contrariorum
et immutatorum verborum habeat, ex quibus et ipsis tamen saepe
non ornatior quam vehementior fit oratio, si in tempore et cum
modo assumantur, turn his, quae verae adfectionis speciem praeferunt, excitetur. [16 ]


ego vero haec lumina orationis velut oculos quosdam eloquentiae
credo, sed neque oculos esse toto corpore velim, ne cetera membra
officium suum perdant 17


item, si raro interseremus has exornationes [scil., ohovotitcotov,

6(ioioteXeutov, Ttapovonaoia] et in causa tota varie dispergemus,
commode luminibus distinctis inlustrabimus orationem. [18 ]

**15** **"Every kind of prose style, the formidable as well as the middle and the plain,**

**16** **"Indeed Demosthenes and Cicero teach us that a perfectly well-made speech is one**

**other hand is inspired by those figures which present the image of true emotion".**
**(Aquila, § 30).**
**"** **"I certainly believe that these highlights of diction are like the eyes, as one might say,**

**66** **SOUND REPETITIONS IN ANCIENT LITERARY THEORY**


Decorations of style above all should never go so far as to detract from
the _gravitas_ of a public speaker:


ita sit nobis igitur ornatus et suavis orator — nec tamen potest
aliter esse — ut suavitatem habeat austeram et solidam, non dulcem
atque decoctam. [19 ]


(One may note that _suavis,_ as distinct from _dulcis,_ apparently refers to
a degree of sweetness compatible with serious oratory.


'gravi' inquit 'ac suavi commotus oratione'. gravitatem ad sapientiam, suavitatem ad eloquentiam referamus: immo gravitatem ad
sententias, suavitatem ad verborum ornamenta referamus. [20 ]


_Dulcis,_ on the other hand, seems to denote a high degree of sweetness,
possibly bordering on the excessive:


quin etiam gustatus, qui est sensus ex omnibus maxime voluptarius
quique dulcitudine praeter ceteros sensus commovetur, quam cito
id, quod valde dulce est, aspernatur ac respuit! [21 ]


That _dulcedo,_ however, was considered compatible with poetry and
perhaps especially associated with rhyming figures may be inferred from
Horace's _Ars Poetica._ The term _dulcis,_ in connection with the aesthetic
aspects of poetry, is mentioned in the conspicuously rhymed couplet
(cf. p. 40).


non satis est pulchra esse poemata: dulcia _sunto_
et quocumque volent animum auditoris _agunto,_ _[22 ]_


It is probably no accident that Horace uses the term _dulcis,_ rather than
_suavis,_ to describe the aesthetic qualities of poetry in contrast to _pulcher,_
which seems to have intellectual or moral rather than aesthetic connota

**puns] sparingly and distribute them variously all over the speech, we shall properly**
**illuminate our style with distinctive highlights." (** _**Auctor ad Herennium,**_ **IV, 22, 32).**
**19** **"Thus we wish that the orator should be ornate and sweet — indeed it cannot be**
**otherwise — that he should have a sweetness which is firm and solid, not soft and over-**
**cooked." (Cicero,** _**De Oratore,**_ **III, 26, 103).**
**20** **" 'Moved', he says, 'by speech weighty and sweet'. Let us associate weightiness with**
**wisdom, and sweetness with eloquence: In fact we should associate weightiness with**
**meaning and sweetness with decorations of diction." (Victorinus,** _**Expl. in Rhet. Cic.,**_
**I, pp. 164-5 Halm).**
**21** **"But even the sense of taste, which is the most luxurious imaginable and in contrast**
**to the other senses is excited by sweetness, how quickly does it reject that which is very**
**sweet and spits it back!" (Cicero,** _**De Oratore,**_ **III, 25, 99).**
**22** **Cf. Chap. III. n. 30.**

tions. According to the scholiast _**pulcher**_ does not mean 'beautiful' in the

Horace's rhymed precept, that in addition to intellectual and moral substance poems also must have aesthetic appeal, may be a reference to
rhyming figures. This interpretation might help to explain the remark of
the _**Auctor ad Herennium,**_ according to whom rhyming figures are lacking
_**in pulchritudo:**_


... in similiter cadentibus... in similiter desinentibus... in adnominationibus... est... lepos et festivitas, non dignitas neque pulchritudo. [24] )


While the sparing use of rhyming figures does not affect the _**gravitas**_
of a serious orator, too many and blatant rhymes may have a ludicruous
effect.

THE USE OF RHYMING FIGURES **69**


quid est enim tam furiosum quam verborum vel optimorum atque

scientia? [31 ]


According to Quintilian the 'imitation' of Cicero's style by some orators
consisted in the futile insertion of Cicero's favorite clausula:


noveram quosdam, qui se pulchre expressisse genus illud caelestis
huius in dicendo viri sibi viderentur, si in clausula posuissent 'esse
videatur'. [32 ]


A competent orator does not, like Morgenstern's weasel, think of the
figure first and then suit the speech to the figure:


illud ad postremum praecipiendum videtur, ne proposita tibi figura,
quaerens elocutionem ei subiungere, cito verba colligas, ut in earn
figuram, quam destinaveris, incidant; infirmum enim hoc et
puerile erit 33


Morgenstern's weasel, on the other hand, may be considered an
extreme example of this rhetorical excess since not only its diction but
even its actions are subordinated to the rhyme:


Das
aesthetische
Wiesel.


Ein Wiesel
sass auf einem Kiesel
inmitten Bachgeriesel.


Wisst ihr
weshalb?


Das Mondkalb
verriet es mir
im Stillen:


**31** **"What, indeed, is as maddening as the empty sound even of the best and most**
**distinguished diction, if neither sentiment nor knowledge are suggested by it?" (** _**De**_
_**Oratore,**_ **I, 12, 51).**
**32** **"I have known certain people who were convinced that they would have beautifully**
**expressed the style of this magnificent speaker if they had finished with the clause 'esse**
**videatur'." (X, 2, 18).**
**33** **"Finally a cautionary remark seems to be in order. One should not, with a figure**
**in mind, try to subordinate the speech to it and quickly connect the words to coincide**
**with the proposed figure; this certainly is unsound and silly** **" (Aquila, § 48).**


**70** SOUND REPETITIONS IN ANCIENT LITERARY THEORY


Das raffinierte Tier
tat's um des Reimes willen. [34 ]


Besides the risk of ridicule, excess of rhyming figures also may create
the impression of artificiality and insincerity, and thus raise suspicion
against the speaker.

Exaggerated attention to style in serious matters does no credit to the
speaker:


at ubi res agitur et vera dimicatio est, ultimus sit famae locus,
praeterea ne decet quidem, ubi maxima rerum momenta versantur,
de verbis esse sollicitum. [35 ]


Acoustic balance and rhyming figures especially may make the speech
appear unnatural or false:

4' "Certainly it is also a sort of versification to give one pattern to the entire speech,
and this on the one hand is the result of manifest effort, which is something one must
especially avoid being suspected of; on the other hand it also creates boredom and
surfeit from monotony. Also it is too sweet, and moreover — one loses credence and
all goodwill and sympathy who is caught in this care, and the judge cannot believe a
man or feel sorry or angry for him who appears to be unmoved." (Quintilian, IX, 4,
143). _Note:_ Quintilian refers to metrical patterns in prose, rather than to rhyming
figures. The passage is part of the conclusion of Book IX which contains a treatise
on the Gorgianic figures.
" "The unscrupulous use of figures especially is viewed with distrust. It evokes the
suspicion of insidiousness, treachery, and fallacy. This is the case if the speech is
addressed to a powerful judge, but most of all if to tyrants, kings, and generals in
prominent positions. Of course a person quickly becomes angry if like a silly child
he is being outwitted by the figures of a skillful speaker...." (Longinus, 190v, 17, 1).


**VI**


A CATALOGUE O F RHYMING FIGURES


**... cum aut duplicantur iteranturque verba**
**aut leviter commutata ponuntur, aut ab**
**eodem verbo ducitur saepius oratio aut in**
**idem conicitur aut utrumque, aut adiungitur**
**idem iteratum aut idem ad extremum refer-**
**tur aut continenter unum verbum non in**
**eadem sententia ponitur; aut cum similiter**
**vel cadunt verba vel desinunt; ... aut cum**
**gradatim sursum versus reditur ... aut cum**
**eiusdem nominis casus saepius commutan-**
**tur.**
**(Cicero,** _**Orator)**_


The following catalogue of rhyming figures includes identical and modified
repetitions of sound involving parts of words, entire words, or groups of
words, in unspecified or consecutive positions; at beginnings and/or
endings of _**cola;**_ or inclusive of whole _**cola.**_
The catalogue was compiled from a broad selection of sources but the
list of authors is by no means complete. Besides standard literary theorists, authors of late or uncertain date were also considered insofar as they
are indebted to the classical tradition and seem to tally with established
authorities. No conclusions are drawn from evidence which appears in
uncertain authors but is not corroborated by standard authorities.
Passages on Gorgianic figures generally suffer from a high degree of
corruption and must therefore be evaluated with caution.
In cases where the example of a figure does not tally with its definition,
a more pertinent example may be contained in a variant reading. For
instance, TIO^UTIXCOTOV in the definition of Zonaios (Walz ed.) is described
in the usual way as a repetition of the same word in several cases:


... oxav o auTog _**Xoyoq noXX&v**_ TI"C<QCTE(BV dvci^ANP&vriTai. [1 ]


**1 "...when the same word takes on many inflections in turn** **" (p. 686, vol. 8,**
_**Rhetores Graeci).**_

A definition may also be followed by a selection of both inadequate and
adequate examples, as in the passage on £7iavd^.r|ii/i<; by Aquila Romanus:


... in hac (figura) ex plurium verborum continuatione repetita idem
quaeritur, ut si dicas: patriae suae quisquam, o dii immortales,
exitium est machinatus? et hoc: _nonpotest_ iam, _nonpotest,_ inquam,
libera haec civitas esse. [4 ]


While the first example does not tally with the definition the second is
pertinent: Halm suspects corruption in this passage.
All the same the first example is not entirely absurd. It illustrates a
connotation of STtavd^riyiq which Aquila does not mention, _viz.,_ that of
'taking up the tale' again after an interruption. The example involves no
repetition. Instead, the continuity of the main clause: _patriae suae quis-_
_quam exitium est machinatus_ ? is interrupted by the exclamation, _o dii_
_immortales,_ and then completed.
Definitions of this more logical than acoustic aspect of £7tavd^r|yiq are
given, e.g., by Phoebammon:

**A CATALOGUE OF RHYMING FIGURES** 75


Aquila's first example is not corrupt in itself but was inserted as a result
of contamination with other sources. This interpretation would tally with
the passage on èjravàkT|V|/i<; by Martianus Capella, who in his treatise on
figures generally duplicates Aquila's catalogue. Martianus quotes
Aquila's second example almost _verbatim_ but omits the first. [7] (A more
detailed analysis of Aquila's corruptions will follow later.)
In other cases identical examples may be used by different authors to
exemplify different figures or effects. These divergencies need not always
be a sign of corruption because several figures may coincide in one
example, or a figure may have several aspects which are defined by
different terms. Corruption must be suspected, however, if a pertinent
example reappears in another context where it makes less sense, as
Longinus's example of euphony resulting from dactylic rhythm :


**touto tò** v|/f|cpia^a xòv xóxe xfj **jtó** ^ **si** Trepiaxavxa **kìvSuvov**
rcapeWJeìv ènoiriaev _&anep_ vécpo<g. [8 ]


For unclear reasons (possibly because several forms of the definite
article occur in it?) this passage from Demosthenes is used by an anonymous author to exemplify no^ùjrxcDxov. [9 ]

Figures involving similar endings, according to Chr. Walz, have the
highest incidence of _lacunae:_


(ó|ioiox£^£uxaìov) haec figura, quae saepius ó|ioioxéA,eoxov
vocatur, plurimas in nostris libris lacunas peperit 10


Since the present catalogue is not an end in itself but a tool intended for
practical use, terminology is not stressed. Figures are classified, regardless
of technical terms, according to their acoustic effects. This method in
some cases may restrict the usual scope of a figure or place it in a category
not normally associated with it. Moreover a modern reader may easily
misjudge acoustic effects in ancient texts because his notions of correct
pronunciation are inadequate.
Even so acoustic qualities must be considered a safer and more
convenient basis of classification than the ambiguous and sometimes
contradictory terms of ancient literary theory.

**8 203v, 39, 4. (Demosthenes,** _**De Corona).**_
_**8 Anon, schem.,**_ **pp. 646-7, vol. 8 Walz.**
**10** **"ónoioieXeuxaìov: this figure, which more often is called ójioioxÉXeuxov, has**
**produced the most** _**lacunae**_ **in our treatises** **" (footnote to Tiberius,** _**schem.,**_ **p. 562,**
**vol. 8 Walz).**


76 SOUND REPETITIONS IN ANCIENT LITERARY THEORY


Grammatical and linguistic terms are not always applicable if acoustic
effects are made the basis of classification. Such terms in the present
catalogue all the same are used, without reference to their grammatical
and semantic meaning, to describe elements of the acoustic structure.
For example, the term 'word ending' in the acoustic sense sometimes does
refer to a grammatical suffix but in other cases may describe a rhyme
effect which includes part of the stem. Conversely, the term 'word' in the
category of 'modifications' may refer to the acoustic identity of the stem
only.
Acoustic criteria admittedly are inadequate for the classification of
rhyming figures which include logical elements, e.g., noXunxoDiov and
icapovonaaia. To classify sound-sense figures as modified repetitions of
sound means doing justice to part of their qualities only. But this onesided treatment seems to be the most convenient solution, unless one
prefers to exclude the whole group of rhyming or rhyme-like sound-sense
figures from the catalogue.


**CATALOGUE**


_I. Repetition in consecutive or unspecified positions_


A. _Word endings_


The two figures which produce similar word endings, _similiter desinens_

(6|ioiot£^.sotov) and _similiter cadens_ (ohoiotctcotov), are often confused.
In most sources the connotation of o i i o i o t e ^ e u t o v is not 'similar word
endings' but rather 'similar endings of coordinated _cola';_ the similarity
may consist in the repetition of entire words as well as word endings.
6|i0i6nTC£)T0v, on the other hand, results from similar cases which may
produce similar endings of words, but it has no specific place relative to
the sentence or verse.
All the same oiioioxeXsuxov is sometimes exemplified by syntactically
or metrically uncoordinated word endings, while 6|xoio7ctcotov may be
used at beginnings or endings of _cola_ where it stresses the rhythm or
metre. Although the term onoiorcxcoxov is mostly limited to words which
have cases, i.e., nouns, adjectives, and pronouns, parallel inflectional
forms of verbs are sometimes included.

The first example of _confine_ (or the variant, _confinies,_ which eliminates

(Concerning the random rhyme effect in noxam -iram in the above

transform the line into another Leonine hexameter:

In this form the line would be a conventional example of ¿iioioxeXeuiov.)

conventional elements of o^oioxe^suxov.)

Corruption likewise may have caused the difficulties of the following
passage:


... 7iapovo|iacria, quae dicitur adnominatio. ea non uno modo
fieri solet: ex vicinia quaedam praedicti nominis ducta casibus
declinatis, ut Domitius Afer pro Cloatilla: "mulier omnium rerum
imperita, in omnibus /rerum/ infelix." et cum verbo idem verbum
plus significans subiungitur: 'quando _homo_ hostis, _homo.''_ _[3]_ _'_ _[7 ]_


The first example in this passage from Quintilian is difficult for two
reasons: (1) The description' _casibus declinatis'_ corresponds to JtoWmrcoTov
rather than to 7tapovojj.aaia. (2) If one accepts the description, the
example, which only contains the different cases _omnium - omnibus_ but
has no derivatives or declensions of a noun, is not very pertinent.
Although nothing in the critical apparatus suggests major corruptions,
one may point out that the first word in Quintilian's first example, _mulier,_
is also the first word in several parallel passages on Tiapovojiaaia with
_double entendre_ on the word _mulier._ (Cf., e.g., Rutilius Lupus, n. 31;
_Carmen de Figuris,_ n. 36.) Moreover Quintilian's second example, which
is based on a double meaning of _homo,_ is paralleled in most other
sources including the Greek authorities.
Identical repetitions with change of connotation also include puns on
proper names:


**7iA**, **okt** ) id est copulatio, in qua idem verbum aut nomen continuo
positum diversa significat ut est 'sed tamen ad illam diem _Memmius_
/erat _Memmius/._ _[36 ]_


ji^oKti, copulatio. ea figura elocutionis, in qua idem verbum aut
nomen, bis continuo positum, diversa significat, ut est illud: 'sed
tamen ad ilium diem _Memmius_ erat _Memmius.'_ ita enim hoc bis
positum est, ut superius quidem nomen tantum significet hominis,
posterius velit intellegi eundem qui semper fuerit ac sui similem. [39 ]

**Besides exactly identical forms, changes of grammatical classification**
**may involve changes in the quantity of vowels in otherwise identical**
**words.**


**adnominatio est cum ad idem verbum et nomen acceditur coramu-**
**tatione vocum aut litterarum, ut ad res dissimiles similia verba**
**adcommodentur. ea multis et variis rationibus conficitur. adtenua-**

40 "What, indeed, could appear more similar to a careless observer than these two
words, _suis_ and _suist_ Which they are not, because one signifies 'to sew' and the other
'sow'. Therefore we grant that they are similar in sounds and syllables, but we understand that as parts of speech they are dissimilar, because one has tenses and the other
has cases; these two things especially set the analogies apart." (Varro, _De Lingua_
_Latina,_ X, 7).
41 "(Transference) ...when the same word is put first in one quality and then in
another, in this way: 'Why do you pursue _(curas)_ so assiduously a matter which will
give you much trouble ( _curas)T_ or: 'It is pleasant to be loved _(amari),_ if one takes care
that nothing of bitterness _(amari)_ be involved.' or: 'I shall come _(veniam)_ to you if the
Senate grants permission _(veniam)_ to me.'" _(Auctor adHerennium,_ IV, 14, 21).
42 "The figure of 'additional meaning' results when the identity of a verb or noun is
approximated with a change in vowels or consonants, so that similar words are applied
to dissimilar things. This figure is produced in many various ways, for example, by
weakening or contraction [?] of the same letter, thus: 'This fellow who is bragging so
proudly and making a show of himself, was sold _(venit)_ before he came _(venit)_ to
Rome', and through the opposite: 'Those whom this man conquers _(vincit)_ by the dice,
he immediately fetters _(vincit)_ with irons.'; by lengthening of the same letter, in this
fashion: 'From here the sweetness of the birds _(avium)_ leads into pathless regions

43 "Otapovonacria, dvaK^aaig) In another way, too, identical sounds either are put
in a different meaning or are modified by protraction or correption only: I actually
wonder that this device, which is frigid even in jokes, should be traditionally taught

44 "Inflection of a noun or a verb either is according to that which signifies genetive
('of something') or dative ('to something') and so on, or according to singular ('one')
or plural ('many'), for example, 'men' or 'man'; or according to mood ('forms of
delivery'), for example, according to question or command; 'did he walk?' or 'walk!'
are inflections of a verb according to these forms." (Aristotle, _Poetics,_ 1457a, XX, 10).
46 "In fact there are two categories of words; one is the productive kind, which by
inflection generates many different forms from itself, such as _read, have read, may read,_
and others after the same fashion; the second is the unproductive kind, which does not
generate anything from itself, such as _also, hardly, tomorrow, rather, why."_ (Varro, _De_
_Lingua Latina,_ VIII, 9).

Besides several cases or inflections of a word, several derivatives of a
stem also produce modified sound repetition. The example of _traductio_
(48, above), partly also belongs to this category, since the form _divitis_ is
not an inflection of _divitiae_ but the genetive of _dives,_ i.e., a derivative from
the same root.

d. _complex modifications._  - Various forms of slightly modified
repetitions may be counted as figures if they result in a pun or give a witty
turn to an otherwise undistinguished statement. These figures are not
based on systematic changes. They occur in individual cases where
acoustic similarities of semantically different words encourage the production of a pun. Sound-sense effects of this type are achieved by slight
metatheses or changes of single letters, or by the addition or subtraction
of letters and syllables.

figure frequently also occurs in ancient authors, in this fashion: 'what is agreeable
_(libet_ ) to him is permitted _(licet)_ to him'. Occasionally, too, the effect is not inelegant

for example: 'you have promoted a bill which did not fail _(deerat)_ you; of course it
was _(erat)_ very carefully written'." (Aquila, § 27).

_ad Herennium,_ IV, 21, 29).

**II.** _**Repetitions**_ _**at Beginnings of Cola**_


**Repetition of word endings, words, or groups of words often occurs at**
**beginnings of parallel** _**cola,**_ **i.e., of coordinated clauses in prose or of**
**corresponding metrical units in poetry.**


**A.** _**Word endings**_


**aliquando, sicut in geminatione verborum diximus, initia quoque...**

B. _Entire words_


_1. repetition in identical form_


Repetition in identical form at beginnings of _cola_ may involve nouns,
verbs, pronouns, and even prepositions and conjunctions. These repetitions frequently introduce parallel constructions of entire _cola_ and may
be combined with rhyme effects at endings.

82 '"Nireus, indeed', says Homer, 'brought three ships, Nireus the son of Aglaia,
Nireus, that most excellent hero.' Clearly the repeated beginning of the speech with
the same name of Nireus, as well as the paratactic arrangement, give the appearance
of a certain fullness to the matter...." (231r, II, 61).
Aristotle quotes the same passage from Homer to illustrate the amplifying effect of
repetition _(Art of Rhetoric_, 1414a, III, 12, 4).
83 "Sappho likewise expresses herself gracefully by means of repetition [at beginnings],

(The _zeugma_ in this example implies the logical termination of each
clause, _moverunt,_ which only appears at the end of the last clause.
Without this economy of words, the end rhyme effect typical for many
forms of _repetitio_ would be achieved in this example. Whether deliberate
or not, the _zeugma_ is appropriate because it reduces the incidence of sound
repetitions in this piece of serious oratory.)

**2.** _repetition in modified form_


Modified repetition at beginnings of _cola_ is frequently the result of
jtoMmxcoxov. This figure consists of several cases or grammatical
modifications of the same noun, pronoun, or adjective at beginnings of
consecutive _cola._ According to some sources rcoMmxcoxov may include
derivatives from the same stem, also defined as _derivatio._


Unlike identical repetitions at beginnings (which often are followed by
entirely parallel constructions of _cola_ with rhyme effects at endings),
**tcoMtixcoxov** does not tend to produce parallelism or end rhyme.


**7io^D3ixcoxov ex pluribus casibus.** **quod c u m saepius initium ab**

**eadem parte orationis fiat, ilia ipsa pars declinationibus aut numeris**

**immutatur per casus, ut est,'** _senatus_ **iussit,** _senatui_ **placuit,** _senatum_

**certum est praecepisse, a** _senatu_ **delectus est.'** **[99 ]**


**"" "... and several sentences vigorously start with the same words immediately** **"**
**(Quintilian, IX, 3, 30).**

**(Martianus Capella, 175G, V, 535).**

**Besides grammatical inflections of the same word, inflected forms of**
**several synonyms also may be arranged at beginnings of coordinated** _**cola.**_
**These forms also belong to the 'figures of many cases' but must be**
**considered as figures of thought rather than sound. The repetition of**
**sound, which in regular jtoWmxcoxov is limited to the acoustic identity of**
**the word stem, vanishes completely in the examples containing synonyms.**

Figures of this type often involve inflected names of persons who share
some special quality or belong in the same historical context:


plura nomina casibus commutatis hoc modo faciunt adnominationem: ' _Tiberium Graccum_ rem publicam administrantem prohibuit
indigna nex diutius in eo commorari. _Gaio Gracco_ similis occisio
est oblata, quae virum rei publicae amantissimum subito de sinu
civitatis eripuit. _Saturninum_ fide captum malorum perfidia per
scelus vita privavit. Tuus, o _Druse,_ sanguis domesticos parietes et
vultum parentis aspersit. _Sulpicio,_ cui paulo ante omnia concedebant, eum brevi spatio non modo vivere, sed etiam sepeliri prohibuerunt.' [103 ]


Other examples of Tro>.u7cioiTov contain adjectives instead of nouns:


... generibus autem hoc modo: ' _pulchra_ autem haec fama continget _pulchrum_ erit exteras nationes existimare _pulchre_
communibus utilitatibus consulemus,' numeris autem sic:
_'grata_ semper singulis fuerunt _gratum_ ille superior Africanus
senatoribus fecit ' (n. 46, p. 85).


The declension of pronouns also may be counted as toMttccqtov. The
acoustic similarity between several cases of a pronoun is often weak.


multiclinatum contra, variantibu' quod fit.
_tu_ solus sapiens, _tibi_ cuncta cedere debent,
a _te_ consilium petere et _tua_ dicta probare. [104 ]


(This example shows that modifications other than simple case inflections
may be included in Tro^uTrrcoxov. The word _tua_ in the last line, although
not an inflectional form of the personal pronoun but of the possessive
pronominal adjective, apparently is also part of the figure. Likewise, in
Aquila's example (46, above) the adverb, _pulchre,_ is combined with
several cases of the adjective, _pulcher.)_

exemplified by Aristotle:

III. _Repetition at Endings of Cola_


A. _Word endings_


In the preceding sections rhyme effects were not classified according to
quantitative prominence. Except for puns involving changes in the
quantity of a vowel (cf. examples, p. 83), no systematic distinction was
made between long and short syllables, or between 'stressed' and 'unstressed' or differently 'stressed' rhyme effects. The exact phonetic nature
of'stress' or acoustic prominence in prose and verse is not well established.
From the definitions and examples in the sources it may not be concluded
with certainty to what extent, if at all, 'stress' influences the quality of a
figure.
Sound repetitions at endings of _cola_ are no exception. Although some
authors seem to associate _similiter cadens_ with equally 'stressed' syllables
and _similiter desinens_ with 'unstressed' syllables, other authorities give
different definitions. In some sources _similiter desinens_ even includes, not
only long, 'stressed' word endings, but also entire words.
The separation in this section of'unstressed' and 'stressed' word endings
therefore must not be considered as an interpretation of ancient literary
theory. On the contrary such a separation is rather artificial and full of
risk. But grouping together figures from naturally or metrically 'unstressed' endings helps to show that such endings indeed may consitute
rhyme.
Rhyming figures resulting from 'unstressed' endings collide with the
theory that deliberate rhymes must contain at least one long, 'stressed'
syllable, as, e.g., in _natus - gratus._ _[110]_ It is true that similar 'unstressed'
suffixes are not as striking in oral presentation as are rhyme effects from
one or more similar 'stressed' syllables. But the examples in this section


10" "¿7iav6Xri*fi<; is the repetition of the same words in a similar position and with
the same connotation, after the insertion of a longer passage; it is done to coincide
with some conclusion of the period, as, for example In Latin this figure is called
'resumption' or 'repetition'." _(ibid.,_ § 5).
110 Example proposed by E.Wolfflin ( _Der Reim im Lateinischeri)._


A CATALOGUE OF RHYMING FIGURES 101


show that even 'unstressed' syllables which in another position might be
elided, such as _-um, -am, -os, -as,_ suffice to produce deliberate end rhyme
effects. Sequences of the same effects in poetry can hardly be dismissed
as 'automatic rhyme'.

112 SOUND REPETITIONS IN ANCIENT LITERARY THEORY


This example from Aquila is duplicated by Martianus Capella. [166 ]

Similar terminations, perhaps intended as an example of avTiaxpocpf],
may be found in Horace's lines:


ut pictura poesis: erit quae, _si propius stes,_
te capiat magis, et quaedam, _si_ long _ius_ abites. [167 ]


The aesthetic aspects of poetry are described by means of rhyming
figures: If one considers that Horace often exemplifies in his diction the
literary devices which he is describing, _pictura_ might be taken as a
reference to rhyming figures.
(Horace's couplet expresses a general maxim attributed to Simonides
and also quoted in other sources, e.g., the _Auctor ad Herennium_ : "poema
loquens pictura, pictura taciturn poema debet esse" (Example of _commu-_
_tatio,_ IV, 28, 39).
In a treatise on poetic craft, however, a rhyming note on painting may
also have more specific connotations. Since Horace does not mention any
technical terms it is not possible to decide whether dviiCTxpocpr), Tiapovo
H a a i a _{stes - abstes_ ) or some other figure is being exemplified. But
generally terms related to painting or embroidering are often associated
with rhyming figures. In ancient treatises words like _color_ and _fucus_ with
their ambivalent connotation of 'decoration' and 'deceit' often refer to
rhyme. _Pictura_ therefore may be a technical reference, and by his rhyming
couplet Horace may have wished to emphasize the colorful effect of
rhyming figures in poetry.)

181 In Aquila's catalogue the three figures of repetition, ¿jtavaA/rmn?, AvaSiicX/Baig,
and 7cpocra7i68oaig, are each exemplified by one pertinent and one incongruous
illustration. The three incongruous examples not only do not correspond to the
respective definitions but have no repetitions in any form. Halm's comment: _plura_
_deesse manifestum est_ (footnote), does not seem to explain the difficulty. It may be
argued, on the contrary, that there are three sentences too many: (1). "patriae suae
quisquam, _o dii immortales,_ exitium est machinatus?" (¿TcavdXrm/i?). Cf. pp. 74-5,
notes 4-7. (2). "concurrerat ad tantae rei nuntium, _sicut commune civitatis periculum_
_monebat,_ senatus frequens, equester ordo universus + + . " (dva8i7tA.co<H<;). Cf.
p. 115, note 180. (3). "haec, _o dii immortales,_ in civitate moliri ausus est Glaucia?"
(jtpocra7i68oai<;). Cf. p. 113, note 170. All three examples contain parenthetic clauses
(italicized), two of which are identical. Contamination seems probable in the case
of £icavdXri\|/i;, which as a figure of thought has the meaning of 'taking up the tale'
after a digression (cf. pp. 74-5). Aquila's seemingly incongruous example would make
good sense as an illustration of this logical aspect of ¿jtavd^rivn;. The same explanation, however, does not apply to the other two figures, neither of which has the connotation of 'digression' or 'parenthetic clauses'. It should be added that Martianus
Capella duplicates Aquila's definitions, as well as the pertinent illustrations to all three
figures, but omits the incongruous examples.
192 "¿Jtavcupopd is the figure [utterance?] which by reduplication shows an extension,
as, for example " ( _schem_ ., p. 598, vol. 8 Walz).

A special form of repetition both at beginnings and endings is the
a sequence of coordinated _cola_ proceeding by 'steps': The
ending of the first _colon_ is repeated at the beginning of the following,
whose ending in turn is repeated at the beginning of the next. Proper
names, nouns, and verbs may be thus repeated. Depending on the
grammatical structure, KXijiaJ; may involve changes of case or inflection,
which reduce the similarity of sound; it may even consist of synonyms
without acoustic similarity. But frequently contains repetitions
in identical form.

into the following member, so that the speech is composed as if from some kind of
steps Thus, for example, Cicero says in the _Pro Milone_ : "Indeed he has committed
himself not only to the people but also to the Senate; and not only to the Senate but
also to the army and auxiliaries of the State; and not to these only " Cicero used
the figure in moderation, however; for in the third 'step' he was content to say "and not
to these only", so as not to repeat the entire phrase Licinius Calvus brilliantly a n d . . .
with felicity (used K>a|ia^) in his speech against Vatinius " (Aquila, § 40). The
example from Cicero's _Pro Milone_ is also quoted by Martianus Capella (176G, V, 536).
188 "(For impressiveness from paratactic arrangements) the figure called ( **kWho** ^)
'gradation' may also be used, as in the passage from Demosthenes: 'I did not say this
without writing a proposal; nor did I write a proposal without undertaking the embassy; nor did I undertake the embassy without convincing the Thebans.' Certainly
the speech almost seems to climb up higher and higher by degrees; on the other hand,
if someone would express the same matter in this form: 'After having spoken and
written a proposal I undertook the embassy and convinced the Thebans', it would be
a simple statement and not impressive at all." (Demetrius, 243r, V, 270).

**Atreus dying left it to Thyestes, possessor of many sheep,**

VI. _Similar Cola_

Figures involving similar length and structure of coordinated members
do not themselves belong to the figures of sound repetition but are
usually mentioned in the same context. Although parallel _cola_ do not
always contain sound repetition they often have ¿(ioiohtcotov and
¿lioioTsXsuxov in parallel positions and thus may result in multiple
rhyme. The following examples (partly used in earlier sections) show
multiple rhyme effects in similar _cola._


haec duo genera (scil., similiter cadens et similiter desinens) quorum
alterum in exituum, alteram in casus similitudine versatur, inter se
vehementer conveniunt; et ea re qui his bene utuntur plerumque
simul ea conlocant in isdem partibus orationis. id hoc modo facere
oportet: 'perditissima ratio est am _orem_ pet _ere,_ pudorem fug _ere,_
_diligere_ for _mam,_ neg _legere_ fa _mam.'_ hie et ea verba quae casus
habent ad casus similes, et ilia quae non habent ad similes exitus
veniunt. [211 ]

211 "These two figures, one of which consists in similarity of endings, the other in
similarity of case, are very close to each other; accordingly those authors who make
good use of them often place them together in the same parts of the speech. This should
be done as follows In this example those words which have cases come to the same
cases, and those which do not come to the same terminations." ( _Auctor ad Herennium,_

118 "But concerning similar _cola..._ the following passage from Isocrates is an example " (Tiberius, _schem.,_ p. 562, vol. 8 Walz).
au «The figure of rópiaov, or nearly similar _cola_ " (Aquila, § 24).
215 "'Member of a sentence' is the term for something expressed in a few words
without utterance of the entire thought; it is answered in turn by another member of
the sentence, in the following manner.... This is one unit which we call a member;
the unit should then be answered by another unit It is possible for this figure to
consist of two members, but in its most satisfactory and complete form it consists of
three members, in the following manner " ( _Auctor ad Herennium,_ IV, 19, 26).

**APPENDIX:**


**THE CATALOGUE OF RHYMING FIGURES EXEMPLIFIED**
**FROM CLASSICAL GREEK AND LATIN POETRY**


With the exception of puns and other figures containing elements of
'thought', examples of rhyming figures described in ancient literary
theory may be readily identified in any classical Latin poem.
The following list of rhyming passages illustrates the figures of the
preceding catalogue by concrete examples from classical Latin poetry.
These passages are chosen almost at random and in most cases could be
substituted by other excerpts from any classical Latin poet.
For the sake of clarity the Latin examples for each figure are preceded
by examples from Greek poetry earlier than artistic prose. These parallels
show that the classical Latin poets could find models for their rhyme
effects in ancient Greek poetry without recourse to handbooks of rhetoric.
If one considers that classical Greek poetry such as Homer's and Sappho's
works belonged to the standard curriculum of a liberal education at Rome,
one may assume that the sound patterns of some Greek poems were


218 "iooKtoXov. **The figure** of **'balanced members' results where the members of a**
**period are equally balanced** **" (** _**Carmen de Figuris,**_ **11. 82-4).**
**818 Cf. p. 104, example 127.**


128 SOUND REPETITIONS IN ANCIENT LITERARY THEORY


familiar to Roman poets. It is likely that the direct imitation of older
poetry in all its aspects also included the imitation of rhyme effects, while
the influence of rules of rhetoric on poetry was probably slight.

hie totum caveae consessum ingentis et ora

Non-rhyming lines in this example of multiple correspondences contain
the 'stressed' rhyming syllable, _or,_ in the fifth foot: _clamoribus implet,_
_corpore virtus._ For similar rhyme sequences and the inclusion of the
penultimate foot in the rhyme pattern, compare other examples from
Homer, Vergil, and others (Part Two).

**Part Two**


**SOUND REPETITIONS**
**IN CLASSICAL LATIN POETRY - EXAMPLES**


VII


I N T R O D U C T I O N


verba loquor socianda chordis.
(Horace)


The preceding survey of ancient literary theory has shown that sound

repetitions by classical standards were neither cacophonous themselves

nor incompatible with poetry. In this section it will be pointed out that

figures of sound repetition in quantitative poetry were even used as

conventional elements of formal composition.

This thesis admittedly collides with accepted critical opinion, according

to which conventional rhyme did not exist in classical antiquity.

Throughout quantitative Latin poetry it may be observed that rhyming

figures not only occur in regular patterns but also depend on a con
ventional repertory of rhymes. Regular arrangements of rhyme in

parallel metrical positions (especially at verse endings) contribute to

overall coherence and continuity, create stanza-like patterns, or form

arrangements of central symmetry. The formality of these patterns is

heightened by the constant recurrence of stock rhymes, which may be

identified in almost any classical Latin poem regardless of author, metre,

and subject.

It is true that not all rhyme effects in classical Latin poetry are of this

ubiquitous type. Some rhymes do have the quality of'figures' in the sense

of exceptional, optional ornaments of diction. But the conventionally

repeated rhymes are frequent enough to appear, almost like metre, as a

fundamental, impersonal, formal element of the poet's craft.

(This is not to say that the conventional rhymes should be dismissed as

mechanical devices for which no artistic competence was required.

Classical Latin poets on the contrary show various preferences and

cultivate various perfections in their use of rhyme. But the individual

variants are slight and hardly affect the basic patterns of conventional

sound repetition.


144 SOUND REPETITIONS IN CLASSICAL LATIN POETRY


Some individual preferences will be exemplified and analyzed later.
These personal characteristics deserve closer study and might warrant a
separate investigation. Typical rhyme patterns, if they could be identified,
would be useful especially as critical tools.)
Although the continuous rhyme patterns are most appropriate for epics
while the stanza patterns have a natural affinity to elegiacs and lyrics,
particular rhyme patterns are not strictly associated with particular
metres. Sequences of rhyming couplets and quatrains occur in epic and
iambic poetry; continuous rhyme sequences may run through a lyric
poem regardless of the division into metrical stanzas. In these cases,
however, several rhyme patterns often occur in combination.
In epic poems the repetition of rhymes or rhyme words at verse endings,
beginnings, and in other parallel metrical positions, often continues over
long passages or throughout the entire poem. These multiple repetitions
occur in various intervals and appear to be arranged with a certain
periodic regularity. In long poems clusters of frequent repetition of a
particular rhyme often alternate with periods of infrequent repetition and
long pauses, after which the clusters reappear.
In many cases the arrangement is complicated by overlapping patterns,
so that more than one correspondence can be found for the same verse
line. For example, the beginning of a verse may correspond to another
beginning seven lines apart, while the ending of the same verse may be
part of an end rhyme sequence of couplets or quatrains. Multiple sound
repetitions create further difficulties. For example, if the word _capilli_ is
echoed not only by _capillos_ but also by _illi_ or _Achilles_, it is not easy to see
how the rhyme sequence should best be diagrammed.
A systematic structural analysis of rhyme patterns in several poets,
with the help of a computer, could probably determine whether epic
rhyme patterns are based on a definite formal system. Since epic poetry
was sung rather than spoken — Achilles, for example, sang heroic songs
to his lyre — one may suppose that epic texts were adapted to musical
compositions in which rhymes coincided with conclusions of musical
phrases. In view of the formality of ancient poetry and its close association with music one might expect that regularly structured sequences of
rhyme periods could be identified.
In the present work definite numerical ratios of epic rhyme sequences
are not established. Regular patterns of end rhyme effects within the
same poem and formal acoustic parallelisms between several poems,
however, are identified through analysis by computer and through manual
analysis respectively. Besides regular periodic and symmetrical runs in


INTRODUCTION 145


_Aeneid_ 1,1 have found acoustic replication, i.e., similar sounds or identical
words, between verse endings in parallel positions not only among the
several books of the _Aeneid_ but also among books or parts of other epic
works by various authors. Replications may also be observed between
parallel lines of different works by the same or different authors.
Some examples are:
1. Vergil's _Aeneid-._ VIII, 61: _honorem,_ XI, 61: _honorem;_ X, 61: _casus,_ XII,
_61: casus._
2. Vergil's _Eclogues_ : IV, 16: i7/is, X, 16: ¡"//as; V, 16: _olivae,_ VIII, 16:
_olivae._
3. Ovid's _Metamorphoses:_ I, 16: _unda,_ VI, 16: _undas;_ III, 16: gere/iiem,
IV, 16: _gentes._
4. Vergil's _Georgics_ and _Eclogues: Geor._ Ill, 1: _canemus, Eel._ IV, 1:
_canamus._
5. Vergil's _Aeneid_ and Catullus LXIV ( _Peleus and Thetis): Aen._ XII, 1:
_Latinos,_ Cat. LXIV, 1: _pinus._
6. Ovid's _Metamorphoses_ and Catullus LXIV: Met. IV, 37: _frequentant,_
Cat. LXIV, 37 _\ frequentant._
(It may be added that, besides exactly parallel replications, syllables
and words also are repeated at nearly parallel verse endings, e.g.: _Eel._ VII,
55: _Alexis,_ II, 56: _Alexis-, Aen._ VII, 30: _amoeno,_ VIII, 31: _amoeno._
Sets of replications moreover are echoed in nearly parallel positions,
e.g., _Eel._ VIII, 82: _lauros, Aen._ VI, 82: _auras; Eel._ VI, 83: _lauros,_ VIII, 83:
_laurum; Aen._ XII, 84: _auras,_ Cat. LXIV, 84: _auris._
These effects will not be included in the present study but they might
be treated in a separate investigation. Nearly parallel repetitions appear
to have some significance for the structure of epic poetry, especially if one
considers that consecutive or nearly consecutive lines of the same epic
poem often carry similar endings.)
Certain results of the manual analysis indicate that the ancient concept
of acoustic similarity may include some 'ablaut' forms, i.e., repetitions
with vowel change such as _bella_ - co//es - _ulla._ As can be seen from the
examples (pp. 223-4), 'ablaut' sets in the computer analysis have in fact
turned out to form regular patterns of repetition.
The replications and other regularities do not themselves elucidate the
sequence of overall repetitions, but they seem to confirm the original
premise of the present investigation, _viz.,_ that classical Latin poetry was
composed according to patterns which involved the ordered repetition of
sound effects.
To underline the conventional character of continuous rhyme patterns,


**146** **SOUND REPETITIONS IN CLASSICAL LATIN POETRY**


examples from classical Latin epics (see below) will be compared with
passages from Homer and Sappho. The similarity of the Latin examples
with ancient Greek rhyme patterns can hardly be overlooked.
An arrangement different from continuous sequences of rhyme is the
stanza-like rhyme pattern. Rhyme schemes comparable to post-classical
rhymed stanzas occur in metrical stanzas as well as in continuous metrical
schemes icaiá crtí%ov.
Rhymes in elegiac and lyric poems often emphasize the metrical
structure. As pointed out by E. Eichner [1] and others, the elegiac couplet,
which naturally falls into four metrical sections, has a strong tendency
to rhyme at caesuras and verse endings. These rhymes create patterns
comparable to those of self-contained, four-line rhymed stanzas in postclassical poetry. Extreme examples of rhyming elegiacs include the
regular repetition of entire half-lines.
Metrical stanzas sometimes rhyme in consecutive or alternating lines
but more frequently carry rhymes in parallel parts of the metrical scheme.
For example, in a Sapphic poem several Adonic clauses may echo one
another, or several strophes in the Alcaic metre may begin with the same
rhyme. Rhymes between corresponding parts of the metrical system often
include the rest of the verse line and are combined with parallel structure
**(íctókcqXov,** 7iapi **(TcoCTi<;)** of entire lines. Probably these parallel arrangements of rhymes correspond to a definite system of musical notations.
On the other hand, strophic effects from rhyme also occur independent
of metrical stanza forms. Epic and iambic poems sometimes contain
strictly regular rhyming passages which have the effect of rhyming
strophic sequences. Refrains ( _versus intercalares)_ likewise impose a
stanza-like periodicity upon uniform metrical schemes.
Patterns of this type occur, for example, in Vergil, who shows a special
preference for regular, quatrain-like sequences of end rhyme. These
arrangements can easily be identified in all of Vergil's authentic works and
also in some uncertain works of the _Appendix Vergiliana._ The _Aetna,_
sometimes attributed to Vergil, contains comparable rhyme schemes.
It is an open question whether quatrain patterns in the _Appendix_ may
be considered a mark of authenticity or whether, on the contrary, they
must be discounted as deliberate attempts by imitators to sound like
Vergil. The doubtful works perhaps were attributed to Vergil because of
their peculiar rhyme patterns.
Besides epic sequences and stanza forms, rhyme effects also occur in


_**1 Bemerkungen über den metrischen und rhythmischen Bau, sowie über den Gebrauch**_
_**der Homoeoteleuta in den Distichen des Catull, Tibull, Properz und Ovid**_ **(Gnesen, 1875).**


INTRODUCTION 147


symmetrical arrangements. The symmetry sometimes results from entire
verse lines ('refrains'), especially when beginnings of poems are echoed
by endings. In most cases, however, the symmetry is achieved by a
combination of various rhyming figures at verse endings. Symmetry of
rhyme is frequent in Catullus's shorter poems and, to a lesser degree,
in Horace.
A special form of symmetry in Catullus is based on regular sequences
of 'vocalic rhyme', i.e., assonance of the last, accented vowels of verse
lines. Vocalic rhyme _(rime vocalique)_ was identified and defined by
N. I. Herescu. [2 ]

Symmetrical patterns of vocalic rhyme, however, are not mentioned
by Herescu and do not seem to be identified anywhere else in the literature.
These arrangements apparently are a specialty of Catullus (they also
occur in some poems of the Priapean collection). In sophisticated
examples the symmetry of the accented final vowels overlaps with other
patterns of conventional rhyme.


The formal quality of the ancient repertory of rhymes is most obvious
if one compares rhymes and rhyme words in poems of different content
and character. As a rule some rhyming figures, especially those involving
proper names, but also repetitions of nouns and verbs, are intimately
connected with the theme of the poem. The words used in these figures
receive added emphasis from their prominent acoustic position and may
properly be called 'theme' words.
On the other hand, a surprising number of rhyme words and rhymes in
classical Latin poetry turn up in almost every context. It may be observed
in particular that certain acoustic sets, each consisting of a metrically
stressed vowel or diphthong and the consonant(s) succeeding it, such as
_-ôr-, -ent-, -ât-, -ïn-, -und-, -urb-_ and words containing such sets, e.g., _ora,_
_dolor es; gentem, parentes; mater, vocatus; pinus, carina; tellus, procella;_
_unda, mundus; urbem, turba_ are used over and over again at verse endings
in most patterns of regular repetition. These words and their echoes can
be identified in practically any poem, whether strictly required by the
context or not.
It is remarkable how little the repertory rhymes change with the
subject and character of the poems. As will be shown by examples
(below) the same acoustic sets and even words reappear in epic, didactic,
and lyric poetry and usually fit well into the context. Minor adjustments
to the theme, however, may sometimes be observed. A case in point is

_1 La Poésie Latine,_ chapter V: L'Assonance, pp. 135ff.


148 SOUND REPETITIONS IN CLASSICAL LATIN POETRY


the _Moretum,_ a simple _epyllion_ describing the preparation of breakfast
in the country. Terms connected with empire and navigation obviously
do not belong to the theme of the _Moretum._ The poet (Vergil?) in fact
omits the conventional rhyme word _carinae_ ('ships') in favor of the more
appropriate _farinae_ ('flour') but manages to accommodate _urbem, orbem,_
and even _undas_ ('waves'), which in this case means 'dishwater'.

Not only the rhyme patterns but also the stock rhymes of classical
Latin poetry might be 'echoes' from older poems. In the _Iliad_ endings
and beginnings of verses are marked by stock rhymes and repetitions of
proper names, such as AxiM-eu? and A%aioi (and inflected forms) at
endings, and Tp&e<; at beginnings. One may assume that _Achilles_ and
_Achates_ at verse endings and _Troes_ at beginnings in Vergil's _Aeneid_ are
direct imitations and imply a reference to the style as well as to the theme
of the _Iliad._ Some of Homer's acoustic effects, including 'ablaut' sets, for
example - £teM.e - _aXkoi -_ pdM-ei _- \izxalXa,_ are echoed

by Latin repertory rhymes, such as _illo - capilli - procella - mella -_
_metalla._


VIII


EPIC CONTINUITY


The following passages from Greek and Latin poetry (selected at random)
show continuous or recurring repetitions of sound in parallel metrical
positions. Most examples illustrate end rhyme effects, but rhyme at
beginnings and in other metrical positions is also represented.
The several types of sound repetitions may easily be identified with the
rhyming figures of ancient literary theory. For example, in the first Book
of the _Iliad_ line endings are connected by UOJIUJITCOTOV, such as the proper
name X%iXXeuc; or the noun Xaoi in various grammatical cases;
6|iovoxe>v£UTOv, as in the similar verb endings \ia%ea9ai - iKzo&ai 8¿xea&zz, etc.; ¿HOIOHTCOTOV, as in the genetive forms Seoio - fiiofo 8epxo¡xevoio, etc.; frequent repetition of the same word (TO aux6 ovo|ia),
e.g., aitoiva; similarity of sound, e.g., epicavre - idvra - exovreg _t_ yevovro - _eovra,_ etc.; or KUVECTCTIV - 9aX,d<xerr|(; - dvacomq _eXeaaiv_
(including 'ablaut' sets).
Similar effects, including the use of identical sounds and even words,
may be identified in the Latin examples.
In addition to overall recurrences of words and sounds adjacent verse
endings are also connected by local rhyme effects; these include similar
unstressed endings (¿laoioxsleuxov), vocalic rhyme, and possibly figures
of thought such as the juxtaposition of the names of two heroes (Agamemnon and Achilles, _Iliad_ I, 11. 130, 131 and 318, 319; Achates and Aletes,
_Aeneid_ I, 11. 120, 121; Achilles and Achates, _Aeneid_ 1,11. 458, 459), two
goddesses (Athena and Hera, _Iliad_ I, 11. 194, 195 and 206, 208), or two
geographic locations (Athens and Crete, Catullus LXIV, 11. 81, 82).
These multiple repetitions not only create coherence and continuity
but also are arranged in regular patterns. Frequent forms are couplets
(a a), repetitions after one, two, or three intervening lines (a x a, a x x a,
a x x x a), and quatrains of various shapes (a a b b, a b a b, etc.). These
groups often occur in combination.

_**Notes:**_ The above register of line endings from _**Iliad**_ I shows that the
various rhyme patterns are for the most part achieved with a limited
repertory of constantly repeated sound effects.
In _**Iliad**_ I, out of a total of 611 verse endings, the proper name AxiM-eix;
(including acoustically dissimilar forms, e.g., AxiA-fjoc;) in various
grammatical cases occurs in lines 1, 7, 54, 58, 84, 121,131,148,215,292,
319, 322, 330, 348, 364, 489, 558; or 17 times.
The name A%ccioi in various grammatical cases occurs in lines 12,15,17,
22, 61, 79, 123, 127, 135, 150, 162, 163, 227, 229, 237, 240, 276, 305, 344,
347, 368, 374, 376, 389, 392,409, 454, 473, 478, 484, 509, 559; or 32 times.
The proper name KnoXX&v in various grammatical cases occurs in
lines 14, 21, 43, 64, 72, 182, 370, 373, 380, 438, 457, 479, 603; or 13 times.
The proper name Ayajienvrav in identical form occurs in lines 94, 102,
130, 172, 285, 318, 335, 355, 411, 442, 506; or 11 times.
Other names repeated at verse endings in _**Iliad**_ I are A$i|VT|, "Hpr|,
'OSuaoeijq, 'OXunrtoq (including acoustically dissimilar forms, such as
'OSocfjoi;, OCXuhtiov 5e), and _**Zsbq.**_
The noun A.aoi in various grammatical cases occurs in lines 10, 16, 263,
375, 382; or five times. The noun fircoiva in identical form occurs in
lines 13, 23, 95, 111, 372, 377; or six times. The noun skcitohPti in
various grammatical cases occurs in lines 65, 93, 99, 142, 309, 315, 431,
443, 447; or nine times.
Other nouns repeated at verse endings are, e.g., (xOSoq, 30|ioc; (which
are mutually convertible by metathesis of the letters ¡ _J_ . and $ and often occur
in combination), and Koupri. Some words which are not nouns also form
sequences, e.g., the pronoun auxoq and various derivatives (auxs,
auTCog), and the verbs etvai, eirceiv.
Acoustic sequences are formed by the verbal endings -aaSai, -eoSai,
-i£co and by the genetive suffix -oio. Metrically unaccented word endings
result in various rhyming combinations.
Frequently repeated 'ablaut' sets are _**-kk-**_ (Axi _**kkevq, &kkr\), -vz-**_

(navTCOv, sysvovro), _-aa-_ ('OSixrceix;, 3a>vd<7a-r|<;), and -or- _(avtuTT],_
fipiorog).
As the computer analysis of _**Aeneid**_ I also suggests, the acoustic
patterns of a specific book or poem apparently are based on a limited
selection of words and sounds in each case. In _**Iliad**_ I certain repertory
words, as well as certain acoustic sets, are used over and over again at
verse endings, while others are used infrequently or not at all. (In some
cases words occurring only a few times are concentrated within the same
area and thus contribute to the acoustic continuity. Examples are

_**Notes'.**_ The above section from the Catalogue of Ships exemplifies a
variety of rhyme effects at verse endings, beginnings, and in other parallel
metrical positions.
Repetition of a final word is exemplified by nxoMe&pov (11. 569, 584),
in combination with parallelism of _**cola**_ : oi Sè Muiaivag el%ov oi
x'fip' AnuK^aq efyov and à|x(pevénovxo in 11. 574, 585 (also echoed by
ScopflGCTOVTO in 1. 587), with parallel constructions neXX/r|VT|v x'efyov
i|5' Aiyiov oi xe Aaav efyov i|S' OixuXov
Repetition of a final word with change of cases occurs in 11. 571, 583:
èpaxeivriv, èpaxeivà?, combined with parallelism of _**cola**_ at beginnings :
'Opveia<; x'èvé|xovxo
The acoustic set _**-zaa-**_ (partly reinforced by further similarities: _**-ri-,**_

_**-co-, -(a)v),**_ is repeated at verse endings in 11. 573: Tovoecoav, 579:
ripcùECTOiv, 581: KTixraeaaav, 582: Méaor|v.
Similar beginnings occur, for example, in 11. 569, 581: oi 8è, oi
5'.... 573, 584, 585: oi ...., oi x'...., ov xe, 576, 586: xffiv
XCÒV
Repetition at beginnings and endings is exemplified by Xaoi Xaoix;
(11. 578, 580).
Parallelism without end rhyme occurs, e.g., in 11. 569, 570: ei>Kxi|ievov
nxoÀieOpov, sÙKxi^évag xe KXecovaq.
Words without acoustic correspondences in this particular passage,
e.g., Aya|ié|iva>v, apvoxoi, _%O.XKÓV_ (11. 576, 577, 578), belong to the
conventional repertory of Homeric verse endings and form rhyming
sequences elsewhere in the poem.

The recurring rhyme effects in Catullus's _epyllion_ are comparable to
Homer's continuous sequences, for example, different cases of proper
names and nouns, such as _Theseus, unda, carina, pinus, amor, mater;_
repetition of grammatical suffixes, such as _-ores',_ sequences of acoustic
sets, e.g., _-aur-_ ; and 'ablaut' sets, e.g., _-ell-, -ill-, -ull-; -ant-, -ent-, -onf,_
_-act, -ect-, -ict-, -auct-._ For similar patterns of continuous end rhyme
effects, the following line endings from Vergil's _Aeneid_ may be compared.

_Notes:_ The above register of line endings from _Aeneid_ I shows sequences
of inflected proper names, e.g., _Achates, Achilles, Dido, lulus;_ sequences
of inflected nouns, e.g. _amor, annus, arma, carina, colonus, harena, ira,_
_orbis, procella, puppis, urbs._ Some of these words also belong to one of
the following sequences: repetition of acoustic sets, such as _-or-_ ; sequences
of 'ablaut' sets, i.e., sets with change of vowels, such as _-act-, -ect-, -ict-,_
_-auct-, -uct-; -all-, -ell-, -ill-, -oil-, -ull-an-,_ _-en-, -in-, -on-, -un-; -ant-,_
_-ent-, -unt-._ These repetitions, as well as repetitions of unstressed endings,

(ojioioTeXeuxa, _similiter desinentia),_ often are arranged in couplets and
quatrains and result in multiple overall connections.
A comparison shows that Vergil's rhyme patterns, and even his sound
effects, are closely related to Homer's sound repetitions. It may be argued
in particular that the sequences _-ass-, -ess-, -iss-,_ etc. (possibly including
_-ax-, -act-1)_ and _-all-, -ell-, -ill-,_ etc., are direct imitations of similar
sequences in Homer.
Likewise, end rhyme effects based on Greek proper names, e.g.,
_Achivi, Olympus, Ulixes,_ appear as direct imitations of Homer's end
rhyme sequences.
The most remarkable instance of direct imitation, however, is Vergil's
end rhyme sequence based on the name of Achilles. In this sequence
Vergil copies Homer's rhyme but not his reason. The corresponding


EPIC CONTINUITY 169


rhyme sequence in the _Iliad_ appropriately stresses the importance of
Achilles as a main character. But while Achilles is a central figure in the
_Iliad,_ he only plays a minor role in the _Aeneid._
One might therefore expect to find end rhyme sequences in the _Aeneid_
emphasizing the names of other, more important characters, especially
Aeneas himself. Yet such is not the case. Throughout the _Aeneid_ the
name of its protagonist appears at beginnings, before middle caesuras,
and in other metrical positions, but never at verse endings. This omission
is the more striking as proper names frequently appear at line endings in
the _Aeneid._ The name of Aeneas, which contains three long syllables,
could be accommodated to the metrical scheme.
Vergil chooses to copy Homer's rhyme rather than create a new rhyme
sequence based on the name of Aeneas. While Vergil avoids the name of
Aeneas at verse endings he includes names of minor figures which, like
the name of Achilles, harmonize with Homer's end rhyme scheme,
notably _Apollo_ (also repeated at line endings in the _Iliad),_ _Camilla,_
_Camillus, Pallas, Sibylla,_ _Themillas._


Rhyme effects and arrangements in the following selection should be
compared with the passages from Catullus and Vergil.

_Note_ : The above selections from various poets illustrate sequences of
rhyming figures in parallel metrical positions. The only exception is the
figure of repetition at both beginnings and endings, exemplified by _possit -_
_possis_ (Lucretius, 11. 1021, 1024) and _vel quod - vel quod_ (Horace, 11. 169,
171). The rhyme effects are arranged in conventional patterns. Some line
endings which have no counterpart in these particular sections, e.g.,
Horace's _annis_ (1. 157) and _iram_ (1. 159) belong to the general repertory
of Latin stock rhymes. While comparable rhyme sequences and line
endings could be identified in almost any passage of classical Latin poetry
selected at random, these features must be considered especially important in a professional dissertation on poetry.

IX


STANZA PATTERNS


**A. ELEGIAC COUPLETS**


_Note:_ As pointed out by E. Eichner, elegiac couplets have a tendency to
rhyme at caesuras and verse endings and thus form self-contained,
stanza-like units. The static effect of these patterns is offset by continuous
overall rhyme sequences at verse endings. In some cases two parallel
runs of rhyme, of hexameter and pentameter line endings respectively,
continue throughout longer passages.


_Catullus,_ _LXXXIIII_


chommoda diceb _at,_ si quando commoda vellet
dicere, et _insidias_ Arrius _hinsidias,_
et turn mirifice sperabat se esse locutum,
cum quantum poterai dixerat _hinsidias._
_5_ credo, sic mater, _sic_ Liber avonculus eius,
_sic_ maternus _avos_ dixerat atque _avia._
hoc misso in Syriam requierant omnibus _aures;_
_audibant_ eadem haec leniter et leviter,
nec sibi postilla metuebant talia verba,
10 cum subito adfertur nuntius horribilis.
_Ionios_ fluctus, postquam illuc Arrius isset,
iam non _Ionios_ esse, sed _Hionios._


_Tibullus, Lib. I, VII: I_

Eichner's rhyme theory, based upon the self-contained, four-part,
stanza-like metrical form of the elegiac distich, is here exemplified by
_isocola_ in first and last 'lines' of the 'stanzas'. The stanza character of
the metrical units is further emphasized by their coincidence with complete units of meaning.
The above selections from different poets show that the elegiac metre
is capable of a wide variety of rhyme patterns. Despite the differences in
treatment, sequences of conventional verse endings may easily be identified in all three selections.


**B.** **LYRIC METRES**


In quantitative lyric poetry rhyme patterns do not correspond to single
verse lines within a strophe but rather to parallel metrical parts of
strophes within a poem. While the end rhyme effects of individual
strophes do not follow a regular pattern, end rhymes throughout entire
poems have a tendency to recur in parallel parts of the metrical scheme.
To some extent the same holds true for rhyme at beginnings and in other
metrical positions. These echoes or 'refrains' emphasize the metrical
regularity of the strophes and at the same time contribute to overall
coherence and continuity.
In the following selections from lyric poetry only those rhyme effects
are pointed out which occur in parallel metrical parts of the strophe
pattern. It will be noted that a number of rhymes are thereby left
unmarked.
