at beginnings in Catullus. Vergil has _colore - colores_ (echoed by amore)
in parallel positions relative to the 'stanza' pattern, and _lauros - laurum_
in consecutive positions at endings; similar beginnings, _carmina_ _-_
_carminibus, necte - necte,_ in parallel arrangements relative to the 'stanza'
pattern; a rhyming incantation in the form of a Leonine verse (1. 80), and
repetition at beginnings and endings : _necte - necto_ (1. 78), _Daphnis -_
_Daphnim_ (11. 83, 84).


X


SYMMETRY


In shorter lyric poems, as well as in short passages of longer poems,
rhyme effects sometimes are arranged in symmetrical form. These
arrangements include conventional end rhymes, similar beginnings,
rhymes in parallel metrical positions, and repetition of entire verse lines;
they largely depend upon a limited repertory of sound effects.
It has been pointed out before that certain acoustic sets, consisting of a
metrically accented vowel and the consonant or consonants succeeding
it, e.g., _-in-, -ell-, -at-, -or-,_ and even certain words containing such sets,
e.g., _pinus, carina; tellus, puella; natus, mater; amorem, honorem,_ are
consistently cropping up at line endings in most patterns of regular
repetition.
A comparative analysis of epic poetry (p. 209) shows that these ubiquitous verse endings tend to occur in parallel positions relative to entire
books or poems by the same or different authors. For example:
1. Vergil's _Eclogues,_ I, 38: _pinus;_ IV, 38: _pinus._
2. Vergil's _Aeneidll,_ 23: _carinis;_ XII, 23: _Latino est._
3. Vergil's _Aeneid_ I, 23: _belli_ ; VI, 23: _tellus._
4. Vergil's _Eclogues_ III, 64: _puella-, Ciris,_ 64: _puellas._
5. Vergil's _Eclogues_ V, 22, 23: _nati, mater;_ Catullus LXIV, 22, 23: _nati,_
_mat rum._
6. Vergil's _Aeneid_ I, 28: _honores;_ IV, 28: _amores;_ VI, 28: _amorem,_ etc.
These findings suggest the possibility that periodic repetitions of sound
in quantitative epic poetry might be arranged according to some definite
numerical scheme. The same might be true of the symmetrical patterns,
but they seem to be an almost exclusive specialty of Catullus.
The following examples (mostly from lyric poetry) show that Catullus
achieves part of his symmetrical effects by means of ubiquitous verse
endings, and that he moreover tends to use certain sounds or words over
and over again in analogous positions relative to the symmetrical scheme.
For example, the acoustic set _-ell-,_ often found in diminutives, and certain
words containing this set, such as _puella, ocelli, pellem,_ are repeatedly


SYMMETRY 191


used in the centre and/or at the extremities of symmetrical poems; the
sets _-ill-, -ull-y_ e.g., in _Septimille, Catulli,_ repeatedly occur in central
positions; and the set _-or-,_ e.g., in _amores, lepore, dolorem,_ is repeatedly
used at extremities.
Besides conventional verse endings Catullus uses a variety of other
devices for his symmetrical effects. Symmetry sometimes results from
repetition of entire verse lines ('refrains'), especially where beginnings of
poems are echoed by endings. In most cases, however, symmetry is
achieved by a combination of various rhyming figures at verse endings
and, to a lesser degree, at beginnings.
A special form of symmetry in Catullus is based on regular sequences
of 'vocalic rhyme', i.e., assonance of the last, metrically stressed (long or
short) vowels of verse lines. Vocalic rhyme ( _rime vocalique)_ was identified
and defined by N. I. Herescu in his book, _La Poésie Latine._ Symmetrical
patterns of vocalic rhyme are not mentioned by Herescu, nor do they
seem to be identified anywhere else in the literature. These arrangements
apparently are a specialty of Catullus.
In most cases the symmetry of the accented final vowels overlaps with
patterns of other rhyming figures. Perhaps the most spectacular example
is the dedication poem (no. 1, _quoi dono)_ to Cornelius Nepos, in which
a sequence of regular, unstressed end rhyme couplets ( _homoeoteleuta_ ) is
combined with a symmetrical sequence of vocalic rhymes.
As may be seen from the illustrations below, none of the various
patterns of symmetry are mathematically perfect. The formal correspondence is always unobtrusive, approximate rather than exact. There can
be no doubt, however, that the symmetrical consonance of these poems
is heard, albeit subconsciously, as a kind of musical echo which results
in harmony and acoustic balance. The sophistication and formal excellence of this acoustic scheme, unrecognized by the critics, nevertheless has
surely contributed to the lasting success of the _Liber Catulli._
The following selections, mainly from Catullus but including works by
other poets, illustrate various devices of sound repetition arranged in
patterns of central symmetry.


**A. REPETITION OF ENTIRE VERSE LINES**


C. 52 : _Quid est, Catulle, quid moraris emoril_
Sella in curuli struma Nonius sedet,
Per consulatum peierat Vatinius :
_Quid est, Catulle, quid moraris emoril_


192 SOUND REPETITIONS IN CLASSICAL LATIN POETRY


The same rather obvious device is used, e.g., in C. 36 ( _Annales Volusi)_ and
several other poems, mostly of invective character. (Possibly Catullus in
these cases also had a visual effect in mind. Ancient examples of visual
symmetry are the _technopaegnia_ of Simmias and others, who wrote short
poems in the shape of an egg, a pair of wings, etc.)

These two examples may be compared for the repeated use of the same
acoustic sets and words in analogous positions of the symmetrical scheme.
In C. 13 the acoustic set _-ell-,_ represented by different inflectional forms
of the word _puella,_ is used near the extremities (11. 4, 11), while in C. 3
the rhyming couplets _puellae - puellae_ (11. 3, 4) and _puellae - ocelli_
(11. 17, 18) occupy analogous positions. The central position of the set
_-ull- (Catulli_ ) in C. 13 (1. 7) is paralleled by the central position of the set
_-ill- {illuc)_ in C. 3 (1. 9). In C. 3 an additional consonance may be intended
in mcrfrem (1. 7) and devoraiis (1. 14). (Apart from the symmetrical
scheme, the verse ending _Cupidinesque_ (13, 12; 3, 1), a rather unusual
form, occurs in both poems.)

In the above examples C. 44 is represented by the concluding passage of
ten lines, while C. 5 is used _in extenso._ In both examples the symmetry
of the vocalic rhymes (cf. right hand columns) largely coincides with the


**196** SOUND REPETITIONS IN CLASSICAL LATIN POETRY


symmetrical pattern of words and acoustic sets used at verse endings
(cf. left hand columns).
C. 44 has the word _legi_ (11. 12, 21) and two inflectional forms of the
same word ( _tussis - tussim,_ 11. 13, 19) at extremities, with the set _-at-,_
represented by the couplet _grates - peccatum_ (11. 16, 17), in a central
position relative to the ten-line sequence.
In C. 5 the set _-or-,_ reinforced by similarity of a further vowel _-i-_ and
_homoeoteleuton -um-_ in _severiorum - basiorum_ (11. 2, 13) is used at
extremities. Adjacent are two inflectional forms of the same word
_(possunt - possit,_ 11. 4, 12). The central position is occupied by the triple
repetition of the word _centum_ (11. 7, 8, 9), also echoed by dormienda (1. 6).
The vocalic rhymes in C. 44 coincide with the words _legi_ (11. 12, 21):
_e; tussis - tussim_ and _fugi_ (11. 13, 19, 14): _u;_ and the set _-at-_ in _grates -_
_peccatum_ (11. 16, 17): _a;_ an additional vocalic correspondence is formed
by _urtica_ and _scripta_ (11. 15, 18): _i._
In C. 5 vocalic rhymes coincide with the set _-or-_ in _severiorum -_
_basiorum_ (11. 2, 13): _o;_ and the word _centum_ echoed by dormienda (11. 7,
8, 9 and 6): _e._ Additional vocalic correspondences are formed by _assis -_
_sciamus_ (11. 3, 11): _a;_ and _brevis lux -fecerimus_ (11. 5, 10): _i._

In this symmetrical introduction to an epic poem (otherwise organized
in stanza-like patterns) the vocalic rhymes partly coincide with the
acoustic sets at verse endings. The symmetry of the passage is further
enhanced by a corresponding arrangement of alliterations and repeated
syllables at beginnings.
The set _-in-,_ represented by the words _pinus - carinae_ (11. 1, 10) is used
for verse endings at extremities of the symmetrical scheme, while the set
_-ell-_ in _pellem_ (1. 5) occupies a central position. The combination _pubis -_


SYMMETRY **197**


_puppi_ (11. 4, 6), although containing different acoustic sets, might also be
considered because it is a frequent example of modified repetition in
rhetorical theory.
The vocalic rhymes coincide with the set _-in- {pinus - carinae,_ 11. 1, 10):
_i_ ; and the combination _pubis - puppi_ (11. 4, 6): _u._ An additional vocalic
correspondence is formed by _undas - currum_ (11. 2, 9): _u._
Symmetry at beginnings partly results from assonance of initial letters
in _Peliaco - pinea_ (11. 1, 10):/? (the word _pinea_ also echoes the line endings
_pinus, carinae_ ); and _cum - caerula_ (11.4,7): _c._ Repetition of initial syllables
occurs in _dicuntur - diva_ (11. 2, 8): _di;_ and _auratam - ausi sunt_ (11. 5, 6): _au._
(For anyone interested in sound-sense correlations it may be worthwhile to note the emphasis on important words produced by the symmetrical scheme: The central words are _auratam...pellem,_ 'the Golden
Fleece', and _ausi sunt,_ 'they dared', while the extremities _pinus,_ 'pine-wood
boats', and _carina,_ 'ship', correspond to each other not only in sound but
in sense as well.)

In this example the symmetrical sequence of vocalic rhymes does not
correspond to any other symmetrical patterns but is combined with a
regular run of unaccented end rhyme couplets _(homoeoteleuta)._
Symmetrical correspondences of accented vowels are: _expolitum - virgo_
(11. 2, 9): _i; solebas - libelli_ (11. 3, 8): _e; Italorum - laboriosis_ (11. 5, 7): o.

some extent interchangeable in Varro's time.

The sounds _e_ and _ae_ in _libellum_ (1. 1) and _saeclo_ (1. 10) respectively were
therefore possibly intended as vocalic consonances.

The following selections from Horace exemplify symmetrical arrangements of conventional rhyme effects without symmetry of vocalic rhyme.
In strophic metres symmetry seems to be frequent in poems which only
consist of two strophes. (Only those rhymes are marked which are part
of the symmetry pattern. Any additional rhyme effects are left unmarked.)

**XI**


THE CONVENTIONAL REPERTORY O F LINE ENDINGS


Classical Latin poets not only use similar rhyme patterns but also similar
rhyme effects, especially at line endings. A limited number of identical
words and syllables at line endings recur in almost any passage selected
at random. In most cases these repertory line endings rhyme with other
line endings in the same poem. Short poems, however, may contain one
or more repertory line endings which have no counterparts. It must be
concluded that apart from the rigors of rhyme a well-defined set of verse
endings belonged to the conventional elements of the poet's craft.
The following random selections exemplify the limited and constant
repertory of poetic Latin line endings throughout various authors, metres,
subjects, and periods. Except for some poems by late or uncertain
authors, the examples are standard selections from well-known poets.


**A.** **VARIOUS AUTHORS**

THE CONVENTIONAL REPERTORY OF LINE ENDINGS **207**


**recurring line endings are frequently those which recur in rhyming com-**
**binations throughout classical Latin poetry.** **[3 ]**

**For example, the following groups of line endings are used over and**
**over again :**


**1.** _**-or- (dolores, amores, honores,**_ **etc.) and 'ablaut' sets.**
**2.** _**-und- (unda, mundus, profundus,**_ **etc.) and 'ablaut' sets.**
**3.** _**-àt- (fatis, late, vocatur,**_ **etc.) and 'ablaut' sets.**
**4.** _**-in- (pinus, carina, Latini,**_ **etc.). and 'ablaut' sets.**
**5.** _**-ent- (parentes, gentes, elementis,**_ **etc.) and 'ablaut' sets.**
**6.** _**-ct-**_ **including vowel changes** _**(acta, ictus, fluctus,**_ **etc.).**
**7. -//- including vowel change (** _**callem, mella, ilia,**_ **etc.).**


- A comparison shows that similar rhyme effects also recur in the regularly rhymed
sonnets of the Italian Renaissance. It is hardly probable that the Italian humanists,
who called their verses 'endecasillabi', would have selected these particular rhymes if
they had not considered them as essential elements of the classical tradition. Some
examples are:


Dante Alighieri. ( _Vita Nuova_, 25).
Ne li occhi porta la mia donna Amore,
Per che si fa gentil ciò ch'ella mira;
Ov'ella passa, ogn'om ver lei si gira,
E cui saluta fa tremar lo core,
Sì che, bassando il viso, tutto smore.
E d'ogni suo difetto allor sospira:
Fugge dinanzi a lei superbia ed ira.
Aiutatemi, donne, farle onore.
Ogne dolcezza, ogne penserò umile
Nasce nel core a chi parlar la sente.
Ond'è laudato chi prima la vide.
Quel ch'ella par quando un poco sorride,
Non si pò dicer nè tenere a mente.
Si è novo miracolo e gentile.


Giovanni Boccaccio. ( _Le Rime_, 3)
Intorn'ad una fonte, in un pratello
Di verdi erbette pieno e di bei fiori,
Sedean tre angiolette, i loro amori
Forse narrando, ed a ciascuna '1 bello
Viso adombrava un verde ramicello
Ch'i capei d'or cingea, al qual di fuori
E dentro insieme i dua vaghi colori
Avvolgeva un suave venticello.
E dopo alquanto l'una alle due disse
(Com'io udi') : "Deh, se per avventura
Di ciascuna l'amante or qui venisse,
Fuggiremo noi quinci per paura?"
A cui le due risposer: "Chi fuggisse,
Poco savia saria, con tal ventura!"


**208** SOUND REPETITIONS IN CLASSICAL LATIN POETRY


Additional examples of ubiquitous line endings may easily be identified.
The random selections show that certain words and syllables are
conventionally associated with line endings in poetry. On the other hand,
not all line endings are consistently repeated. The question remains
whether conventional verse endings alternate with non-conventional
ones, or whether every verse ending in classical Latin poetry belongs to
a conventional repertory.
If an all-inclusive repertory of Latin verse endings exists, it could
probably be identified by a systematic statistical analysis of all extant
classical Latin poems. Research of this type, however, which seems hardly
feasible without data processing equipment, would go beyond the scope
of the present work. Repertory verse endings, as well as related phenomena, shall be studied in a separate investigation.


**Francesco Petrarca.** _**(Le Rime,**_ **197).**
**L'aura celeste che 'n quel verde lauro**
**Spira, ov' Amor feri nel fianco Apollo**
**Et a me pose un dolce giogo al collo**
**Tal che mia libertà tardi restauro,**
**Po quello in me che nel gran vecchio mauro**
**Medusa quando in selce transformollo :**
**Né posso dal bel nodo ornai dar crollo,**
**Là, 've il sol perde, non pur l'ambra o l'auro;**
**Dico le chiome bionde e '1 crespo laccio**
**Che si soavemente lega e stringe**
**L'alma, che d'umiltate e non d'altro armo.**
**L'ombra sua solo fa '1 mio cor un ghiaccio,**
**E di bianca paura il viso tinge:**
**Ma li occhi hanno vertù di farne un marmo.**


XII


COMPARATIVE ANALYSES OF END RHYME PATTERNS


_Note:_ The following analyses both concentrate on epic poetry. Acoustic
replication, i.e., similarity of verse endings in parallel positions relative
to entire books or poems, between various works by the same or different
authors was identified by manual procedures. Patterns of certain
recurrent verse endings in _Aeneid I_ were identified by computer.


**A. ACOUSTIC REPLICATION**


As has been pointed out before, the ubiquitous character of certain verse
endings clearly indicates that they belonged to the standard professional
elements of poetic composition. It appears, moreover, that specific
acoustis effects were associated with definite positions or functions within
the poem.
The analysis of symmetrical poems by Catullus (pp. 192ff.) has shown
that specific acoustic effects repeatedly appear in analogous positions
relative to the symmetrical scheme (for example, the 'ablaut' set -11-, and
even words like _puella, Catullus,_ repeatedly occupy central positions, while
the sets _-ist-, -or-_ repeatedly occur at extremities). This acoustic replication
may be a Catullan specialty without general applications but it has an
apparent parallel in epic poetry.
By writing out the verse endings of several epic poems in parallel form
one discovers that some acoustic sets and entire words tend to occur in
analogous positions.
For example, line 1 (line le) of _Aeneid I, oris,_ is echoed by _Ciris,_ 1 _:_
_amore,_ and _Moretum,_ 1 _: horas._ Line 1 in _Aeneid XII, Latinos,_ is echoed
by Catullus LXIV, _1: pinus._ Line 7 in Vergil's _Eclogue VI, bella,_ is echoed
by _Eclogue X, 7: capellae,_ and _Georgics 1,7: tellus._ Other correspondences
in the same position are: _Eclogue III, 7: memento,_ Ovid's _Metamor-_
_phoses III, 7 parentis, VI, 7: gentis; Metamorphoses_ _V, 7: undis, Ciris, 7:_


210 SOUND REPETITIONS IN CLASSICAL LATIN POETRY


_mundi._ Line 23 in _Eclogue V, mater,_ is echoed by _Georgics II, 23:_
_matrum,_ and Catullus LXIV, _23: matrum._ Other correspondences in the
same position are: _Eclogue IV, 23: flores, VIII, 23: amores, Georgics IV,_
_23: calori; Aeneid II, 23: carinis, XII, 23: Latino est", Eclogue IX, 23:_
_capellas, Aeneid I, 23: belli, VI, 23: tellus._
Similar phenomena appear in Greek epic poetry. For example, line 1
of _Odyssey VI, 'OSvaasV(_ is paralleled by _VII, I : 'OSuaosvq,_ and _IX, 1:_
_'OSvaasoQ._ Line 50 in _Odyssey I, IkxXdaarjz,_ is echoed by _Odyssey VIII, 50:_
_Qahaooav._ Other correspondences in the same position are _Odyssey V,50:_
_ndvzcp,_ and IX, 50: _eovra._
The repeated acoustic sets moreover frequently occur in standard
combinations, such as _pinus - carina, herba - superbas, gentes - parentes,_
_bellum - tellus,_ or in the form of identical radicals and even proper
names: _centum - centum, capellae - capellae, Latino - Latinos, Orpheus -_
_Orpheus._
It is true that with the exigencies of metre the choice of possible syllables
or words at verse endings is limited; also, replications involving grammatical similarity, such as par _entes -_ fur _entes,_ die _abo -_ sacr _abo,_ might be
discounted as accidental effects of grammar and syntax.
Accidental replication seems unlikely, however, in combinations which
involve part of the stem, e.g., _auri -_ h _auri, lollas - Apollo._ But while even
these effects might be dismissed as accidental, the same can hardly be said
of those replications which involve repetition of radicals. It is scarcely
probable that replications of roots, entire words, and proper names
should repeatedly occur by accident, even if one assumes that the poet's
choice of line endings was limited.
The replications of radicals in parallel positions of books or parts of
the same work, or, still more surprising, in different works by the same
or different authors, suggest that recurrent end rhymes in epic poetry are
not distributed at random but arranged according to a definite system
of formal composition.
The present analysis is based on standard (Oxford and Teubner)
editions of Vergil's canonical corpus, with cross-references to the _Ciris_
and the _Moretum;_ further examples include Catullus LXIV ( _Peleus and_
_Thetis)_ and Ovid's _Metamorphoses_ I-VIII. The analysis covers the first
100 lines of each book or poem; those of Vergil's Eclogues which are less
than 100 lines long are used _in extenso._ Examples from Homer's _Odyssey_
are added for contrast and comparison.
For the purpose of the analysis similarity of verse endings is defined
as similarity of acoustic sets (i.e., similarity of the metrically stressed


**COMPARATIVE ANALYSES OF END RHYME PATTERNS** 2 1 1


vowel or diphthong and the consonant(s) succeeding it in the sixth foot
of the dactylic hexameter line), e.g., _iato_ - nafi. This minimal condition
may be reinforced by other correspondences, e.g., similarity of the last,
unstressed vowel (and succeeding consonants, if any), as in _iati - nati,_
and/or identity of the radical, e.g.,/aii -/aium, _nato - nato._ Replications
involving sets with different second consonants such as _certo_ - cernis,
arcus - arma are not included.
For the sake of clarity the following conspectus of replications has been
subdivided into two sections, 1: _Replications involving identical roots_
(except for those which do not satisfy the conditions of acoustic similarity
defined above, e.g., the 'ablaut' set _fuisset - esses_ or the derivation
_pudorem - pudendis_ ; although this type seems frequent enough to warrant
consideration as a possibly deliberate phenomenon. On the other hand,
those replications were included which involve acoustically similar but
semantically and grammatically different roots, e.g., oris - _or&_ (ora, _ae,_ f.
'region', and _os, oris,_ n. 'mouth', pi. 'face') and _aures - auro (auris, is,_
f. 'ear', and _aurum, i,_ n. 'gold')). 2: _Replications based on acoustic_
_similarity of verse endings according to definition_ (including grammatical
similarities and repetitions of radicals).
While the lists of replications of radicals are considered definitive, the
comprehensive lists of acoustic replications possibly have to be enlarged
and modified. It may be observed, e.g., that specific groups of acoustic
sets are paralleled by other groups of sets which differ only by 'ablaut',
such as: be//a - cape//ae - te//us, co//es - _Apollo;_ or: lanas - _canos,_
mwwus - prw«ae.
These combinations suggest that several single sets with different
vowels but with identical consonants might have the quality of an acoustic
group. For example, be//a - co//es - tilla; lanas - carina - mwmis;
'OSotraeix; - KT **|T(&Ecaav** - MXaacrav, occurring in parallel positions,
would then be considered as replications. As will be shown later (see
pp. 223-4), some 'ablaut' sets have been tentatively introduced in the computer analysis of _Aeneid_ I and have turned out to be arranged in regular
patterns.
As long as no overall patterns of replications have been established,
however, the relative value of 'ablaut' combinations and other marginal
cases of similarity, such as identical radicals without acoustic correspondence, must remain an open question.

**B. PATTERNS OF RECURRING END RHYMES IN AENEID I**
**A PRELIMINARY COMPUTER ANALYSIS OF SOME ACOUSTIC SETS**


In order to determine the frequency and distribution of specific acoustic
sets used at verse endings, a preliminary computer analysis of _**Aeneid I**_
was made.
The most frequent sets in this book are -or- (as in oris, dolores, honores),
which occurs 59 times at verse endings; _**-ent-**_ (as in gen/em, vends,
pareniem), which occurs 40 times; and _**-at-**_ (as in penaiis, Achaiae, tour),
which occurs 28 times.
A total of 205 different acoustic sets are used in _**Aeneid**_ /, which
contains 776 lines (not counting lines la-Id). Many of these sets appear
only once or a few times. The average occurrence of acoustic sets other


COMPARATIVE ANALYSES OF END RHYME PATTERNS 223


than _-or-, -ent-,_ and _-at-_ is only 3.2 times. (By comparison, _Eclogue VII,_
for example, in a total of 70 verse lines does not contain any lines ending
in _-or-_ or _-ent-.)_
The runs of the three most frequent sets and various 'ablaut' sets in
_Aeneid I_ show local regularities in the form of consecutive, alternating,
and symmetrical repetitions of intervals, e.g., 4, 4, 1, 1; 47, 5, 5, 47 (-or-);
26, 26, 11, 32,11 _(-at-);_ 3, 3, 3, 2, 32,4, 5, 5 (-r-); 13,19, 7,1, 7, 13, 7 (-<-).
These patterns do not seem to converge towards any general scheme of
periodic repetition, but their regularity can hardly be explained away as a
result of accident. Further analysis will be required to determine the
significance of the repetition of intervals in the end rhyme patterns of epic
poetry.


_Note:_ The numerals in the following examples refer to the number of
verse lines intervening between two consecutive occurrences of the same
acoustic set or 'ablaut' set respectively, including the second occurrence.
For example, the difference between line le _(oris)_ and the next occurrence
of the set _-or- (labores)_ in line 10 is _9;_ the difference between line 10 and
the next occurrence of the set _-or- (dolores)_ in line 25 is _15,_ etc. Besides
regular repetitions and arrangements of intervals, it will be noted that
certain intervals are concentrated in specific sequences. For example, the
interval 23 occurs four times in the sequence _-ent-,_ but not in _-at-;_ conversely, the interval 26 occurs four times in _-at-,_ but not in _-ent-._
